Special Kähler geometries of 𝒩 = 4 {\mathcal{N}}=4 superYang-Mills Preprint: IFT-UAM/CSIC-25-103
Philip C. Argyres, Antoine Bourget, Julius F. Grimminger, Matteo Lotito, and Mitch Weaver

Abstract
The low energy effective theory on the moduli space of vacua of 4d superYang-Mills (sYM) theory defines a special Kähler geometry. For simple sYM gauge algebras, 𝔤 {\mathfrak{g}} , we classify all compatible special Kähler structures by showing that they are in one-to-one correspondence with certain equivalence classes of integral symplectic representations of the Weyl group of 𝔤 {\mathfrak{g}} . We further demonstrate that, for principal Dirac pairing, these equivalence classes are in one-to-one correspondence with the S-duality orbits of the global structures of the corresponding 𝔤 {\mathfrak{g}} sYM gauge theory, after a mistake in the field theory literature is corrected. This provides a low-energy test of S-duality. We also discuss twisted product geometries made from factors with special Kähler structures with non-principal Dirac pairings.



1
Introduction and summary

Information-rich and often exactly computable observables of a supersymmetric quantum field theory are the leading terms in the low-energy effective action of the massless degrees of freedom on
{\mathcal{M}}
, its moduli space of vacua.
In the case of 4d theories with
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
supersymmetry this moduli space is a continuum of states, and the low-energy effective action is encoded in a singular complex geometry on the moduli space.
With more supersymmetry the moduli space geometries become more constrained, and therefore more amenable to classification.

In this paper we classify all possible moduli space geometries of theories with
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
superconformal symmetry and a weak coupling limit described by a super-Yang-Mills (sYM) theory with simple gauge algebra,
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The answer to this problem was seemingly given long ago
Seiberg:1997ax
as the orbifold

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}={\mathbb{C}}^{3r}/(W({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}^{3}),

(1)

where
W({\mathfrak{g}})
is the Weyl group of the gauge group, and
r\doteq{\rm rk}({\mathfrak{g}})
is its rank.

W({\mathfrak{g}})
is a finite group acting via its defining real reflection representation on
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
, “triply complexified” by tensoring with
{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
.
The metric on
{\mathcal{M}}
is the one inherited from a flat metric on
{\mathbb{C}}^{3r}
.
But the moduli space has more structure than just its Kähler orbifold geometry indicated in (
1
).
It has a
special Kähler (SK) structure
,
1
1

1

In the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
class, it is a generalization of an SK structure, called a “triple-SK structure” in
Argyres:2019yyb
, where it is described in detail.

whose definition and relation to the low energy effective action is reviewed in appendix
A
.
In this paper we ask and answer the question of how many distinct SK structures are compatible with the Kähler structure (
1
) of the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM moduli space.

We show (appendix
A
) that SK structures on
{\mathcal{M}}
are classified by integral representations,
S
, of the complexified Weyl group which are symplectic with respect
J
, the Dirac pairing on the charge lattice of the low energy theory; and by a compatible symmetric
r\times r
matrix
{\bm{\tau}}
of low energy
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
gauge couplings.
We then classify and show how to construct all such triples
(J,S,{\bm{\tau}})
, and thereby all possible
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM SK structures.
While the integral irreducible representations of Weyl groups are well-studied, for this application we need to classify their reducible but not necessarily decomposable representations, a module extension problem whose solution is summarized in table
3
.

These
(J,S,{\bm{\tau}})
structures all come in continuous 1-parameter families which we call
SK structure orbits
.
For
J

principal
(i.e., the ordinary symplectic form), and for each Weyl group, each line in second column of table
1
represents one of these orbits, which itself represents a 1-parameter family of equivalent SK structures.
The 3rd column of the table is a certain modular group, called the self-duality the SK structure orbit, that is defined by the SK structure orbit and encodes the geometry of its 1-dimensional parameter space as the modular curve formed by quotienting the Siegel-upper half space
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
by this subgroup (which are reviewed in appendix
C
).

Summary of classification results for
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory SK structures.
In each row of the 2nd column, the (set of) curly brackets represent an inequivalent
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
SK structure for each Weyl group,
W
.
They are determined by
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
orbits of pairs
({\mathbb{Z}}_{s}^{X},n)
that define integral, symplectic representations of
W
and, as explained in the text, are used to label SK structures.
Their first entry refers to subgroups of the center of a corresponding Lie algebra and the second entry refers to an element of their corresponding Ext group, which is summarized in table
3
.
The set of pairs
({\mathbb{Z}}_{s}^{X},n)
enclosed in curly brackets are all
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalent.
The
teal
arrows indicate further
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalences that incorporate Weyl group automorphisms; they act within an SK structure orbit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5938,0.6797}{\lcirclearrowright}}\{\cdots\}
or between them
\{\cdots\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5938,0.6797}{\leftrightarrow}}\{\cdots\}
.
The 3rd column gives the self-duality group of each orbit as a modular subgroup (table
196
).
The circular arrows
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5938,0.6797}{\lcirclearrowright}}
are instances of self-equivalence on the SK structure orbit, so they contribute to the self-duality group of that orbit, so we color the group in
teal
to reflect this fact.
SK orbits or self-duality groups with
✗
’s indicate those cases where either the number of orbits or the self-duality group, respectively, does not agree with the field theory S-duality conjectures as stated in
Kapustin:2005py
;
Aharony:2013hda
;
Bergman:2022otk
, c.f. table
6
.

How do these results compare with the predictions from
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM field theory?
While the local space-time dynamics of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM is parameterized by a choice of gauge algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
and complex gauge coupling
2
2

2

Not to be confused with the boldface matrix
{\bm{\tau}}
of low energy couplings on the moduli space!

{\tau}
, as absolute QFTs they also carry discrete “global structure” data
Aharony:2013hda
that is sensitive to the global topology of the space-time.
This data can be expressed in terms of lattices of charged line probes of the low energy theory on the moduli space which have a principal Dirac pairing
J

Gaiotto:2010be
.
Furthermore, the conjectured S-duality of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM acts on these global structures to combine them into 1-parameter families of
S-duality orbits
.
These S-duality orbits have been worked out in detail for most simple
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
Aharony:2013hda
;
Bergman:2022otk
.

Our results in table
1
find that, for a given Weyl group
W
, the number of SK structure orbits (describing the inequivalent moduli space geometries) and their self-duality groups agree with the predictions from field theory S-duality, except in those cases marked with
✗
’s in the table.
These disagreements come in two types that are correlated with the column that they appear in.
The first type (second column) is a disagreement in the number of SK orbits, and the second type (third column) is a disagreement in the self-duality group.
For the disagreements in all of the
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{2k}
cases, they are due to a mistake in the field theory literature of not properly taking into account the (outer) automorphism interchanging spinor and conjugate-spinor representations.
For the
A_{1}
and
BC_{r}
cases, they have larger self-duality groups, and we interpret this as resulting from the moduli space geometries simply failing to distinguish between inequivalent absolute
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
field theories because they lack sensitivity to do so as an IR observable.

This latter disagreement is not a contradiction: there is no
a priori
reason that a low-energy observable such as the moduli space geometry should be different for all microscopically inequivalent field theories.
Presumably, if we consider more data from the field theory beyond the moduli space SK structures that we have classified, such as the BPS charge lattice of massive states out on the moduli space, then this discrepancy between S-duality groups would be resolved.
Conversely, there is no
a priori
reason that all geometries that we find should actually correspond to moduli spaces of QFTs.
We might lack knowledge of a set of sufficient physical consistency conditions to impose on the geometries to render them physical.
Evidence that we are not lacking such knowledge, therefore, is that we find that each constructed geometry does occur as the moduli space of an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory.

Our results can also be seen as a test of the field theory S-duality conjectures because we compute low-energy observables and their duality structure without direct reference to the S-duality structure of the field theory.
We provide a detailed comparison between the relevant duality data (given by the number of duality orbits and the self-duality group of each orbit) in section
4
.
We find almost complete agreement with the field theory results and provide explanations where discrepancies occur.

In general, how the geometry of the moduli space of vacua of an SCFT reflects and is derived from the operator content of the field theory is a vexed question.
This paper gives a concrete case of this relationship where both sides (field theory and moduli space) are well-studied.
Table
2
highlights the distinctions between conformal field theory (CFT) observables and properties of the moduli space geometry; it also serves to introduce some terminology we use in the rest of the paper.

The asterisk

is to indicate that the S-duality orbits and SK structure orbits are found to be in 1-to-1 correspondence only after a correction to the list of global structure S-duality orbits, described in section

, is incorporated.

Outline of the paper.

Appendix
A
derives the properties the SK geometry of the moduli space of an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory must obey.
Much of this discussion is probably well-known to experts.

Section
2
describes our procedure for classifying all possible
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM SK structures.
We keep the discussion general by not assuming that
J
is principal.
Some of the mathematics we need concerning representations of finite groups over the integers is summarized in appendix
B
.

Section
3
is devoted to explaining our results for principal Dirac pairing, summarized in tables
1
and
3
.
A Mathematica notebook automating these calculations for every Weyl group is available from the authors upon request.
We prove our results for the most intricate case, the
A_{r}
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{su}(r{+}1)
) series of Weyl groups for all
r
, in section
3.1
.
We do not actually prove these results for the
BC_{r}
and
D_{r}
series of Weyl groups, but instead report the pattern found by brute force computation, though a proof along the lines of section
3.1
should not be too hard, just lengthy.
We provide detailed examples in section
3.2
of the calculations for
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{so}(12)
and in section
3.3
for
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{so}(5)=\mathfrak{sp}(4)
, illustrating the peculiar features that occur in these cases.

Section
4
compares our results to the field theory predictions of S-duality, and discusses the relationship between global structures of the field theory and SK structures of the geometry.

Section
5
raises the question of whether there are “exotic” principally polarized CB geometries corresponding to
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM with non-simple gauge algebras which are not just the product of the simple ones.
This hinges on finding reducible symplectic representations which are indecomposable, and involves the use of non-principally polarized symplectic representations as building blocks.
We show how
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{u}(N)=\mathfrak{u}(1)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(N)
gives an example of this, albeit with a free factor.

Further directions.

While considering geometries that are consistent with unbroken
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
supersymmetry, some obvious questions are:

\blacktriangleright

The relative field theories of all
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories have non-principal Dirac pairing on their BPS charge lattice
Argyres:2022kon
.
Is there always a unique non-principally polarized SK geometry corresponding to this minimal/“relative” version of the field theory?
More generally, do all non-principally polarized Weyl group geometries have physical interpretations?

\blacktriangleright

Are there other examples besides the
\mathfrak{u}(N)
ones of section
5
of “exotic” principally polarized geometries built on reducible (non-principal) symplectic representations of Weyl groups?

\blacktriangleright

How are generalized global symmetries, such as
1
-form symmetries and non-invertible duality defects, reflected in the moduli space geometry, and how do the geometries change upon discrete gauging of these symmetries? (See
Bourget:2018ond
;
Argyres:2018wxu
;
Arias-Tamargo:2019jyh
for a related discussion.)

\blacktriangleright

If we drop the requirement of a weak-coupling limit, are there other non-Weyl group isotrivial SK orbifold geometries compatible with
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
supersymmetry?
Are there non-orbifold isotrivial geometries? (See
Argyres:2019ngz
for a related discussion.)

The project of classifying and constructing isotrivial moduli space geometries can also be extended to the more difficult cases with unbroken
{\mathcal{N}}{=}3
or
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
supersymmetry.
Many of the above questions also apply to these theories, or, in some cases, must be addressed.
In particular, such constructions promise to shed light on the analog of “global structures” in non-lagrangian theories where there is no notion of gauge group.

Note added:

After the completion of this work, the paper
cecotti2025
appeared, and we received a preliminary copy of
Moscrop:2025mtl
, both of which have overlap with this paper.
Where our results overlap, they agree.

2
Integer representations of Weyl and complexified Weyl groups

2.1
Ingredients of an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
SK structure

We collect and briefly describe some ingredients of SK structures of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
CB geometries that are needed to describe their classification in the rest of this section.

For a given Weyl group
W=\mathrm{Weyl}({\mathfrak{g}})
for a simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
(with Cartan subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}
), and for a given integral symplectic form
J
on
{\mathfrak{h}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{2r}
, we consider pairs
(S,{\bm{\tau}})
where

\blacktriangleright

S:W\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
is an integral, symplectic representation that is
{\mathbb{R}}
-equivalent to two copies of the fundamental reflection representation of
W
,

\blacktriangleright

{\bm{\tau}}
is an
r\times r
matrix in the Siegel upper half-space such that
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
, i.e., for all
w\in W
,
S(w)\circ{\bm{\tau}}={\bm{\tau}}
, given in (
A.1
). This presupposes that we work in a basis where
J
takes the skew-block form (
5
).

We then define the concept of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM SK structures associated with
{\mathfrak{g}}
and fixed choice of
J
as follows:

\blacktriangleright

An
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
SK structure
for Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
and integral symplectic form
J
is an equivalence class of pairs
(S,{\bm{\tau}})
, defined as above, under the equivalence relation

\displaystyle(S,{\bm{\tau}})

\displaystyle\sim(MSM^{-1},M\circ{\bm{\tau}}),

for all

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}).

(2)

\blacktriangleright

An
SK structure orbit
is an equivalence class of just the integral symplectic representation
S
, i.e., without specifying a compatible value of
{\bm{\tau}}
.

These definitions and their motivation are described in detail in appendix
A
.
A brief summary is that the SK structure of a rank-
r

{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM moduli space, is specified by an
r
-dimensional complex orbifold

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbb{C}}^{r}/W_{\mathbb{C}},

(3)

that carries a canonical Kähler structure but is also equipped with some extra structure that comprises a special Kähler structure.
Here
W_{\mathbb{C}}\subset\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
is the complexification of the Weyl group, a finite group acting linearly, holomorphically, and faithfully on
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
.
Because
W_{\mathbb{C}}
is a reflection group, it is generated by reflections, elements
r_{I}\in W_{\mathbb{C}}
of order 2 which fix a codimension 1 hyperplane in
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
.
The collection of these hyperplanes is the fixed point set of the orbifold action, and
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
is the smooth, non-simply connected component of
{\mathcal{C}}
that is obtained by removing this fixed point set from
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Part of the extra structure is the rank-
2r
lattice,
{\Lambda}
, of electric and magnetic charges of states and probes under the low energy
\mathrm{U}(1)^{r}
gauge group.
This lattice carries an integral symplectic form,
J
, giving the Dirac pairing between charges.
The charge lattice may suffer a monodromy
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
upon being dragged around the fixed point hyperplanes of
W_{\mathbb{C}}
along some closed path
{\gamma}
.
3
3

3

Here
J^{\vee}\doteq J^{-t}
denotes the inverse transpose; the reason for its occurrence is explained in appendix
A
.

These are basis changes of the lattice which preserve the symplectic pairing
J^{\vee}
according to the matrix relation
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{t}({\gamma})J^{\vee}S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})=J^{\vee}
.
Such monodromies define an integral symplectic representation
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
of
\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})
into
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
that we call the
monodromy map
.
The image
\mathrm{Im}S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\subset\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
of the monodromy map forms a subgroup we call the
monodromy group
.
As shown in appendix
A
, the monodromy group is isomorphic to the orbifold group
W_{\mathbb{C}}
as abstract groups, so it is generated by the reflection elements,
r_{I}\in W_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Through this isomorphism, we obtain an integral symplectic representation of
W_{{\mathbb{C}}}
that we simply denote by
S
as used in the above definition of an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
SK structure (orbit).
Since
W_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of the real reflection group
W_{\mathbb{R}}\subset\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
, relative to a real basis of
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
,
W_{\mathbb{C}}
is the reducible
2r
-real-dimensional representation
W_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus W_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Thus, for an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM CB, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\cong_{\mathbb{R}}W_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus W_{\mathbb{R}},

(4)

or, in words, the monodromy group
\mathrm{Im}S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
defined by the integral symplectic representation
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
must be equivalent over the reals to two copies of the real reflection representation of the orbifold group.

A
symplectic basis
of
{\Lambda}
is a choice of splitting into lagrangian sublattices (“magnetic” and “electric”) with respect to
J
,
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}_{m}\oplus{\Lambda}_{e}
, with respect to which
J
is skew block off-diagonal,

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\bf j}\\
-{\bf j}^{t}&0\end{pmatrix},

(5)

with
{\bf j}
an integral
r\times r
matrix.
Even with the matrix form of
J
fixed, there is still a large group of elements
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\subset\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
generating basis changes of
{\Lambda}
that preserve
J^{\vee}
under the action
J^{\vee}\mapsto M^{t}J^{\vee}M=J^{\vee}
which implies
J\mapsto MJM^{t}=J
.
We define this group to be the
EM duality group
of the low-energy theory on
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Two integral symplectic representations,
S
and
S^{\prime}
, satisfying (
4
) and with respect to Dirac pairings
J
and
J^{\prime}
taking the same matrix form (so they have the same invariant factors) in a choice of their symplectic bases, are
integrally equivalent
,

\displaystyle S\cong_{\mathbb{Z}}S^{\prime},

(6)

iff there is an
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
S^{\prime}(w)=MS(w)M^{-1}
for all
w\in W
.
We call
M
an
integral intertwiner
of the representations
S
and
S^{\prime}
, each of which define SK structure orbits.
Therefore, two integrally equivalent symplectic representations of the Weyl group describe the same SK structure orbit, since they differ only by a symplectic change of charge lattice basis which is an EM duality transformation of the low-energy effective action.

We must actually use a slightly more general notion of integral equivalence of symplectic representations than the one described above and in (
2
).
For any
reflection automorphisms
of the Weyl group,
{\phi},\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
, then
S\cong_{\mathbb{Z}}S^{\prime}
if there is an
M^{({\phi},\psi)}\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
S^{\prime}({\phi}\circ w)M^{({\phi},\psi)}=M^{({\phi},\psi)}S(\psi\circ w)
for all
w\in W
.
In this case we say
M^{({\phi},\psi)}
is an integral
twisted intertwiner
.
Denote the set of these twisted intertwiners which intertwine a representation
S
with itself by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{S}}_{S}\doteq\bigcup_{{\phi},\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)}\bigl\{M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\ |\ (S{\circ}{\phi})\,M=M\,(S{\circ}\psi)\bigr\}.

(7)

The structure of this set is described in more detail in section
2.7
, where we show that it is an image in
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
of elements of the
SK orbit self-duality group
under corresponding self-intertwiner maps, which we also define there.

Relative to a choice of symplectic basis, (
5
), the low energy effective action also specifies at each point on the moduli space an
r\times r
complex matrix of gauge couplings,

\displaystyle{\bm{\tau}}

\displaystyle\in{\mathscr{H}}_{r}\doteq\{\,{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}}),\ {\bm{\tau}}={\bm{\tau}}^{t},\ \mathrm{Im}{\bm{\tau}}>0\,\}.

(8)

{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
is the Siegel upper half-space of degree
r
.
The EM duality group acts on
{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
via a fractional linear action (see appendix
A
) which we denote
{\bm{\tau}}\mapsto M\circ{\bm{\tau}}
for
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Two
{\bm{\tau}}
related in this way are equivalent since they differ only by a change of basis.
In the case of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM moduli spaces, the SK structure is
isotrivial
, which means that
{\bm{\tau}}
is constant over the moduli space.
Thus the action
{\bm{\tau}}\mapsto S\circ{\bm{\tau}}
of the monodromy group must leave
{\bm{\tau}}
invariant.
Call the fixed point set of this
S
action
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
.
Therefore, for
{\mathcal{C}}
isotrivial we must have

\displaystyle{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{r}.

(9)

Fixed point sets of integrally equivalent representations are
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
conjugate.
The twisted self-intertwiners,
\widetilde{\mathscr{S}}_{S}
, induce identifications on
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
.
We call
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
modulo these identifications the
conformal manifold of
S
, denoted
{\mathscr{C}}(S)
, in light of its expected connection to SCFTs.
It is the moduli space describing the inequivalent SK structures with a given SK orbit S, i.e., a given symplectic representation equivalence class.
The group of identifications on
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
is the
self-duality group of the SK orbit
S
; we will see that for
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
moduli spaces, they are parameterized by
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
matrices which form discrete subgroups of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories with simple gauge algebra have one exactly marginal complex coupling.
Thus we expect
{\mathscr{C}}(S)
to be one-dimensional.
The conformal manifold of the set of SK structures with a given
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
J
is the disjoint union
\coprod_{i}{\mathscr{C}}(S_{i})
of all integrally inequivalent SK orbits
S_{i}
.

In summary, an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
moduli space SK structure is determined by an equivalence class of pairs
(S,{\bm{\tau}})
under EM duality transformations as claimed in (
2
).
All other properties of the moduli space geometry (its special coordinates and its metric) are determined by the
(S,{\bm{\tau}})
equivalence class, as explained in appendix
A
.

Thus, to classify all
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM moduli spaces, for each gauge Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of rank
r
we need to compute all integrally inequivalent
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
representations
S
of
W_{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}\otimes W({\mathfrak{g}})
, the complexified Weyl group, and also compute all
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
.
We show how to carry this out in the rest of this section.
For most of this section we keep our discussion general by not making any assumption on
J
.
But, along the way, we will see that, modulo the overall normalization of
J
which cannot be determined in a scale-invariant isotrivial SK geometry,
4
4

4

The normalization of
J
can be determined by turning on mass deformations, as was shown, for example, in rank 1 SCFTs
Argyres:2015ffa
;
Argyres:2015gha
.
Also, an induction argument in the rank
Amariti:2024yjg
constrains the allowed
J
to a finite list at each rank for theories with characteristic dimension
Cecotti:2021ouq
not equal to 1 or 2.
there are only a finite number of allowed inequivalent polarizations.

2.2
Classifying integral symplectic representations of a Weyl group

In this section, we provide an explicit algorithm which, given a Weyl group
W
and a symplectic pairing
J
, fully classifies the corresponding
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
SK structures. We summarize here the steps and the main results, referring to the subsections below for the details.

\blacktriangleright

Step 1:
W
-invariant lattices.
We construct a finite number of
W
-invariant lattices
\Gamma_{A}
for
A\in{\mathcal{I}}
an index set. These are in one-to-one correspondence with
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence classes of
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
representations of
W
which are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivalent to the reflection representation.

\blacktriangleright

Step 2: A sufficient class of symplectic integral representations.
Given two such representations
A,B\in{\mathcal{I}}
we construct a family of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
representations
S_{(A,B;D)}
of
W
, which depend on a
binding

D
, and show that every SK structure contains at least one
(S,{\bm{\tau}})
where
S\sim_{{\mathbb{Z}}}S_{(A,B;D)}
.

\blacktriangleright

Step 3: Equivalence class of bindings.

It is then enough to classify
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence classes of these
S_{(A,B;D)}
representations.
We proceed as follows.
Given
A,B\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we find for which
D,D^{\prime}
are
S_{(A,B;D)}
and
S_{(A,B;D^{\prime})}

{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalent. We show that the set of equivalence classes is parametrized by an extension group
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}W}(R_{A},R_{B}^{\vee})
that we can explicitly compute. Hence we introduce a new finite index set

{\mathfrak{I}}\doteq\bigsqcup\limits_{A,B\in{\mathcal{I}}}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}W}(R_{A},R_{B}^{\vee})\,.

(10)

Elements of
{\mathfrak{I}}
are denoted by

\boxed{\mathchoice{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.75pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{

(11)

labels of Weyl reps
R_{A}
,
R^{\vee}_{B}
element of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}W}(R_{A},R^{\vee}_{B})

where we are now taking
D
to stand for an equivalence class of bindings, which is an element of the corresponding
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}W}
group.
(We have boxed and annotated this definition because it is a notation we employ extensively in the rest of the paper.)
For each
{\mathcal{A}}
, its fixed point set in the rank-
r
Seigel half space,
\mathrm{Fix}(S_{\mathcal{A}})\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
, is 1-dimensional (as expected).

\blacktriangleright

Step 4: Integral equivalences of symplectic representations.

We construct explicitly a family of maps labeled by a pair
({\mathcal{A}}_{i},{\mathcal{A}}_{j})\in{\mathfrak{I}}\times{\mathfrak{I}}

{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{j}}:\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\to\mathrm{Sp}(2r,{\mathbb{R}})

(12)

such that for all
w\in W
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{I}}
, and
{\gamma}_{a}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, they satisfy intertwiner and morphism properties,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}(w)

\displaystyle=S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}(w){\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})\,,

(13)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma}_{1}){\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}({\gamma}_{2})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}({\gamma}_{1}{\gamma}_{2})\,.

\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
acts by fractional linear transformations on the natural coordinate,
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
, parameterizing
\mathrm{Fix}(S_{\mathcal{A}})
.
These maps and (
13
) thus encapsulate the algebra of equivalences among SK structures
(S_{\mathcal{A}},{\bm{\tau}})
.

In particular, this allows us to parameterize the set of intertwiners implementing integral equivalences between
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
by

{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\doteq{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}^{-1}(\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}))\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}}),

(14)

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}^{-1}
denotes the preimage in
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.
It is fully characterized in terms of divisibility conditions on the
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
matrix elements.
We show that the set of intertwiners is exactly

\{M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})|MS_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}=S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}M\}={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}),

(15)

which is to say,
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
intertwines
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
iff there exists a
{\gamma}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
such that
M={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})
.
Thus the maps
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}(\cdot)
capture all purely inner intertwiners between
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
,

So we define an equivalence relation on
{\mathfrak{I}}
by

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\sim{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\neq\emptyset\,.

(16)

Then the set of SK structure orbits associated with
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
J
are in bijection with
{\mathfrak{I}}/\sim
.
So, in particular, for fixed
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
J
,

\#\,\textrm{SK structure orbits}\quad\equiv\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\#\textrm{connected components of}\\
\textrm{the conformal manifold of SK}\\
\textrm{structures with given ${\mathfrak{g}}$ and $J$}\end{array}\quad=\quad|{\mathfrak{I}}/\sim|\,.

(17)

\blacktriangleright

Step 5: Self-duality groups of SK structure orbits.

For a given SK structure corresponding to
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathfrak{I}}
, the set of integer self-intertwiners implementing self-equivalences,

{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}\doteq{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}^{-1}(\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}))\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}}),

(18)

is a
group
.
In particular, it is a discrete subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
that parameterizes the similarity transformations of an SK structure which act trivially on
S_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Thus, it is by definition the
self-duality group of the SK structure orbit

S_{{\mathcal{A}}}
, or, for short, the
S-duality group of

S_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
This definition of
{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
, as well as the definition (
14
), will have to be modified to include intertwiners twisted by reflection automorphisms as in (
7
).
This modification is explained in appendix
A.3
and in section
2.7
.

This notion of self-duality group of an SK structure orbit is related to, but in principle distinct from, the self-duality group of the associated
{\mathcal{N}}=4
sYM theory.
As explained in section
4
, we find the two do not always agree, and such cases are marked by red
✗
’s in the summary table
1
.

The results of the computation of the SK structure orbits and their self-duality groups are summarized for simple
{\mathfrak{g}}
and principally polarized
J
in table
1
.
Section
3
discusses the details of these computations.
In particular, for an explicit example of the calculations, the reader is urged to look at the
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{so}(12)
example worked out in section
3.2
.

2.3
Weyl group invariant lattices

Our first task is to list
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence classes of
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
representations of
W
which are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivalent to the reflection representation of
W
.
These are in one-to-one correspondence with lattice representations, i.e. representations formed by restricting the reflection representation of
W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
to
W
-invariant lattices in
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
, as a change of basis for a lattice is given by a matrix in
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and have been classified by Feit
feit1998integral
.
5
5

5

Note that
feit2003some
corrects an error in the classification of
feit1998integral
.

Hence,
Step 1
amounts to reviewing the classification of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence classes of rank-
r

W
-invariant lattices from which we can construct the corresponding lattice representations.
We first introduce some notation.

Table 3:
For each Weyl group (1st column, using Killing-Cartan notation) are listed the subgroups of the center of the corresponding Lie algebra(s) (2nd column).
The 3rd column gives the names from Lie algebra theory of the corresponding Weyl-invariant lattices.
The 4th column computes the Ext group (
37
) of bindings between
R_{A}
and
R_{A}^{\vee}
, and labels each Ext group element for use in table
1
.
Some subgroups
A
appear twice in the 2nd column as
A
and
A^{\vee}
when the dual lattice
{\Gamma}_{A}^{\vee}
is not integrally equivalent to
{\Gamma}_{A^{\prime}}
for any subgroup
A^{\prime}
.
Also, equivalences between subgroups/lattices that are due to outer reflection automorphisms of the Weyl groups are shown using
\simeq
.
The 2nd column should be seen as attributing a name/label to each distinct lattice given by its corresponding subgroup with additional notation to distinguish lattices with identical subgroups.

\blacktriangleright

Consider a simple real Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of rank
r
,
{\mathfrak{h}}
a Cartan subalgebra, and
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}
its linear dual.
We denote by
K:{\mathfrak{g}}\times{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
the Killing form, which is a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form.

K
will also denote the induced inner products on
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}
.

\blacktriangleright

We pick a basis
({\alpha}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,r}
of simple roots, and denote by
{\Gamma}_{\textrm{root}}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}
the lattice they generate.
The long roots are normalized such that they have norm squared equal to 2, i.e.,
K({\alpha}_{\rm{long}},{\alpha}_{\rm{long}})=2
.
Let
(\varpi_{i})_{i=1,\dots,r}
be the corresponding basis of fundamental weights,
6
6

6

Recall that to each root
{\alpha}
is associated the one dimensional eigenspace
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
, and there is a unique element
H_{\alpha}\in[{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha},{\mathfrak{g}}_{-{\alpha}}]
such that
{\alpha}(H_{\alpha})=2
.
The fundamental weights are defined uniquely by the relation
\varpi_{i}(H_{{\alpha}_{j}})={\delta}_{ij}
.

and
{\Gamma}_{\rm{weight}}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}
the lattice they generate.

Z\doteq{\Gamma}_{\rm{weight}}/{\Gamma}_{\rm{root}}
is the center of the simply connected Lie group with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

\blacktriangleright

The Weyl group
W
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{O}({\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast})\cong\mathrm{O}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
generated by reflections about hyperplanes perpendicular to the simple roots.
We call
w_{i}
the (simple) reflection about the simple root
{\alpha}_{i}
.
This defines an action of
W
on
{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
that renders
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}
an irreducible representation of
W
that we call the
reflection representation
and denote by
R
.
It is a real orthogonal representation with respect to the positive definite scalar product
K
.

\blacktriangleright

For any subgroup
H\subseteq Z
, there is a lattice
{\Gamma}_{H}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}
such that
H={\Gamma}_{\rm{weight}}/{\Gamma}_{H}
.
There is also a corresponding dual lattice
{\Gamma}_{H}^{\vee}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}
, where by dual we mean with respect to the scalar product
K
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccl}{\Gamma}_{\rm{weight}}\doteq{\Gamma}_{\{1\}}&\supseteq&{\Gamma}_{H}&\supseteq&{\Gamma}_{Z}\doteq{\Gamma}_{\rm{root}}\\
{\Gamma}_{\rm{coroot}}\doteq{\Gamma}_{\{1\}}^{\vee}&\subseteq&{\Gamma}_{H}^{\vee}&\subseteq&{\Gamma}_{Z}^{\vee}\doteq{\Gamma}_{\rm{coweight}}\end{array}

For simply laced algebras the dual lattices do not provide new lattices; in particular,
{\Gamma}_{\rm{weight}}\cong{\Gamma}_{\rm{coweight}}
and
{\Gamma}_{\rm{root}}\cong{\Gamma}_{\rm{coroot}}
.
For non-simply-laced algebras, however, the dual lattices can give non-isomorphic representations.

\blacktriangleright

The restrictions of the representation
R
to the lattices
{\Gamma}_{H}
and
{\Gamma}_{H}^{\vee}
constructed above, for subgroup
H\subseteq Z
, provide
integral
representations of
W
that we call respectively
R_{H}
and
R_{H}^{\vee}
.
These representations are all equivalent over
{\mathbb{R}}
but they may or may not be equivalent over
{\mathbb{Z}}
.

\blacktriangleright

If we pick a basis
\bm{{\gamma}}^{H}=({\gamma}^{H}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,r}
of
{\Gamma}_{H}
, we can associate to it the dual basis
\bm{{\gamma}}_{H}=({\gamma}_{H}^{i})_{i=1,\dots,r}
defined by
K({\gamma}^{H}_{i},{\gamma}_{H}^{j})={\delta}_{i}^{j}
.
When expressed in these bases, the representations
R_{H}
and
R^{\vee}_{H}
induce explicit group morphisms
W\to\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
that we denote
R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}^{H}}
and
R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}_{H}}^{\vee}
,

\displaystyle R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}^{H}}:w

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{Mat}_{\bm{{\gamma}}^{H}}(R(w)),

\displaystyle R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}_{H}}^{\vee}:w

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{Mat}_{\bm{{\gamma}}_{H}}(R(w)).

(21)

Then we have
R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}_{H}}^{\vee}(w)=\left(R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}^{H}}(w)\right)^{-t}
.

\blacktriangleright

To lighten the notations, we assume a choice of basis
\bm{{\gamma}}^{H}=({\gamma}^{H}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,r}
of
{\Gamma}_{H}
has been made once and for all, and we suppress it from the notations.
Furthermore, we index the set of inequivalent representations and invariant lattices by some label set
{\mathcal{I}}
, so
R_{A}
for
A\in{\mathcal{I}}
runs over the inequivalent
R_{H}
and
R^{\vee}_{H}
for all
H\subset Z
, and similarly for lattices
{\Gamma}_{A}
along with their chosen bases,
\bm{{\gamma}}^{A}
.
We also denote the inverse transpose of a matrix by a
∨
superscript,

\displaystyle R_{A}^{\vee}\doteq R_{A}^{-t}.

(22)

Thus
R^{\vee}
denotes what is commonly called the dual or contragredient representation to
R
.

\blacktriangleright

Since the set
\{R_{A},A\in{\mathcal{I}}\}
has, by definition, all inequivalent representations, the contragredient set
\{R^{\vee}_{A},A\in{\mathcal{I}}\}
does too, though generally permuted and relative to different bases.
In the case of simply-laced Lie algebras, we can (and do) choose representatives
A\leftrightarrow{\Gamma}_{H}
, i.e., without using any dual lattices
{\Gamma}^{\vee}_{H}
.
But for non-simply-laced Lie algebras there are necessarily be inequivalent dual representations,
R^{\vee}_{H}\ncong_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{H^{\prime}}
for any
H^{\prime}
.
Consequently in these cases there are distinct labels
A\leftrightarrow R_{H}
and
B\leftrightarrow R_{H}^{\vee}
, and their associated representations are contragredient,

\displaystyle R_{A}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\doteq R_{A}^{-t}=\left(R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}^{H}}\right)^{-t}=R_{H,\bm{{\gamma}}_{H}}^{\vee}=R_{B}\,.

(23)

The full list of lattices is given in table
3

feit1998integral
;
feit2003some
.

\blacktriangleright

We call
I_{AB}
the matrix of the identity map acting on
{\mathfrak{h}}
, mapping from
{\mathfrak{h}}
in basis
\bm{{\gamma}}^{B}
to
{\mathfrak{h}}
in basis
\bm{{\gamma}}^{A}
. Similarly, we call
K_{AB}
the matrix of the Killing form in bases
\bm{{\gamma}}^{A}
and
\bm{{\gamma}}^{B}
.
(In particular, the subscript
AB
labels these matrices and not their matrix components.)
This means we have the matrix equations,

\displaystyle I_{AB}R_{B}(w)=R_{A}(w)I_{AB}\,,\qquad\text{for all }w\in W,

(24)

and

\displaystyle K_{AB}R_{B}(w)=R^{\vee}_{A}(w)K_{AB}\,,\qquad\text{for all }w\in W.

(25)

Thus
I_{AB}
and
K_{AB}
are intertwiners (a.k.a.,
W
-equivariant maps) between
R_{B}
and
R_{A}
or
R_{A}^{\vee}
, respectively.
We use the defining properties (
24
) and (
25
) of these intertwiners extensively in all computations to follow. Note that

\displaystyle I_{AA}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{r},

\displaystyle I_{AB}^{-1}

\displaystyle=I_{BA},

\displaystyle K_{AB}^{t}

\displaystyle=K_{BA},

(26)

and

\displaystyle I_{AB}I_{BC}

\displaystyle=I_{AC},

\displaystyle K_{AB}I_{BC}

\displaystyle=K_{AC},

\displaystyle K^{\vee}_{AB}K_{BC}

\displaystyle=I_{AC},

(27)

but, in general,
I^{t}_{AB}\neq I_{BA}
,
K^{-1}_{AB}\neq K_{BA}
and
I_{AB}K_{BC}\neq K_{AC}
.

The identification of the integer representations, or, equivalently, invariant lattices are familiar from gauge theory. But in application to non-lagrangian theories a description in terms of gauge theory does not exist, so we are interested in their properties without regard to any chosen basis.
The intertwiner matrices,
I_{AB}
and
K_{AB}
, defined above carry this information.
By defining them as the matrices of the identity map and Killing form on
{\mathfrak{h}}
with respect to our conventional lattice bases
\bm{{\gamma}}^{A}
and
\bm{{\gamma}}^{B}
, we have chosen a particular normalization for
I_{AB}
and
K_{AB}
.
Basis changes of the
{\Gamma}_{A}
and
{\Gamma}_{B}
lattices change
I_{AB}
and
K_{AB}
by multiplication on the left and right by arbitrary
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
matrices.
Thus invariant information in the individual intertwiner matrices can be encoded by normalizing them to integer matrices by dividing them by the rational gcd of their entries, i.e.,
K_{AB}/\mathrm{gcd}(K_{AB})
and
I_{AB}/\mathrm{gcd}(I_{AB})
.
Then, by multiplication on the left and right by
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
matrices they can be put in unique invariant factor form (a.k.a., Smith normal form), for example,
K_{AB}/\mathrm{gcd}(K_{AB})\sim{\rm diag}\left\{1,f_{1},f_{1}f_{2},\ldots,\textstyle{\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}}f_{i}\right\}
, for positive integers
f_{i}
.
(The first entry is 1 because we have factored out the gcd.)
Note that this implies that
K^{\vee}_{AB}/\mathrm{gcd}(K^{\vee}_{AB})\sim{\rm diag}\{1,f_{r-1},f_{r-1}f_{r-2},\ldots,\textstyle{\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}}f_{i}\}
, and that

\displaystyle n_{AB}\doteq\mathrm{gcd}(K_{AB})^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{gcd}(K^{\vee}_{BA})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}}f_{i},

(28)

is an integer invariant.
We will see below that this invariant governs the S-duality groups of many of the SK structures associated to the pair of representation
(R_{A},R^{\vee}_{B})
.

Similar definitions apply to the
I
intertwiners.
Furthermore, since, by definition,
R_{A}\ncong_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{B}
for
A\neq B
, it follows that
I_{AB}
cannot be invertible over the integers for
A\neq B
, and thus their invariant factors cannot all be 1.
Conversely, if
A=B
, then
I_{AA}=\mathbf{1}
, and its invariant factors are all obviously 1.

We emphasize, however, that the invariant factors of the
I
’s and
K
’s do not exhaust the invariant information about the representations that they encode.
For example, the
{\Gamma}_{A}
and
{\Gamma}_{B}
basis changes which put, say,
I_{AB}
in invariant factor form need not put
K_{AB}
in that form, nor allow
I_{BC}
or
K_{BC}
to be put in that form by a basis change of
{\Gamma}_{C}
.
Thus there is much more invariant information in the whole set of intertwiners than in their individual invariant factors.

2.4
Symplectic representations of Weyl groups and their fixed points

We now perform
Step 2
of the method outlined in section
2.2
, that is, we construct a sufficient class of symplectic integral representations.
Consider an integral symplectic form
J
of the block skew-diagonal form (
5
), where the skew block,
{\bf j}
, is a non-degenerate
r\times r
matrix with integer coefficients.
The group
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
is the group of matrices
M\in\mathrm{Mat}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
M^{t}J^{\vee}M=J^{\vee}
.
7
7

7

As reviewed in appendix
A
, it is
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}
, and not
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}
, which governs how the special coordinates and
{\bm{\tau}}
transform under EM duality transformations.
The two matrix groups are related by the inverse transpose of their elements, i.e.
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
iff
M^{-t}\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

The usual, or
principally polarized
, symplectic group is recovered by taking
{\bf j}=\mathbf{1}
, in which case
J=J^{\vee}
.

Introduce for
A,B\in\mathcal{I}
, following
curtis1966representation
, a
binding
of
R_{A}
with
R_{B}^{\vee}
, which is a map
L_{(AB,D)}:W\to\mathrm{Mat}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
of the form

\displaystyle L_{(A,B;D)}(w)

\displaystyle\doteq R_{A}(w)I_{AB}D-I_{AB}DR_{B}^{\vee}(w)\ \in\ \mathrm{Mat}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})

(29)

for all
w\in W
for some symmetric matrix

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=D^{t}\ \in\ \mathrm{Mat}(r,{\mathbb{R}}).

(30)

Note that a non-zero binding is not a group morphism.
And to each binding we associate the symplectic representation

\boxed{\begin{aligned} S_{(A,B;D)}:W&\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\\
w&\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}R_{A}(w)&L_{(A,B;D)}(w)\\
0&R_{B}^{\vee}(w)\end{pmatrix}\end{aligned}}\ ,

(31)

with
J
given by (
5
) with

\displaystyle{\bf j}=I_{AB}.

(32)

Interpreted as the Dirac pairing in the low energy theory on the CB,
{\bf j}
is integral (the Dirac quantization condition) and its invariant factors are physical observables.
On the other hand,
I_{AB}
is generally not an integral matrix as we have defined it.
But its normalization does not affect the SK geometry since
J
only enters linearly in the definition of the EM duality group.
We are thus free to normalize it to be integral by multiplying it by
n/\mathrm{gcd}(I_{AB})
for any non-zero integer
n
, though we cannot determine the value of
n
by our methods.
So we ignore the physical normalization of
{\bf j}
and just take it to be (
32
) from now on.

The fact that (
31
) is a representation follows upon noticing that

\displaystyle S_{(A,B;D)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1\ \ &I_{AB}D\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}R_{A}&0\\
0&R_{B}^{\vee}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\ \ &I_{AB}D\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(33)

That it is symplectic follows from direct calculation, where Schur’s lemma implies that
S_{(A,B;D)}
preserves
J^{\vee}
iff
D-D^{t}\propto K_{BB}^{\vee}
.
It then follows from (
26
) that the factor of proportionality vanishes and so
D
must be symmetric.

A theorem, reviewed in appendix
B
, implies that every SK structure has a representative of the form
S_{(A,B;D)}
given by (
31
).
More precisely, it says that any representation
W\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{R}})
that is
{\mathbb{R}}
-equivalent to the direct sum of two copies of the fundamental reflection representation is
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalent to some
S_{(A,B;D)}
.
So it is sufficient to determine when two
S_{(A,B;D)}
are integrally equivalent to find all the inequivalent symplectic representations of
W
.

The condition that
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
is easily translated, using the action (
A.1
) of
S
on
{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
, to the condition that for all
w\in W
,
R_{A}(w)({\bm{\tau}}+I_{AB}DI_{BA}^{\vee})=({\bm{\tau}}+I_{AB}DI_{BA}^{\vee})R^{\vee}_{A}(w)
.
So, by Schur’s lemma and (
25
), there exists a complex number
{\tau}
(not to be confused with the
r\times r
complex matrix
{\bm{\tau}}
) such that
{\bm{\tau}}+I_{AB}DI_{BA}^{\vee}={\tau}K_{AA}^{\vee}
, implying
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
is the one-complex-dimensional set,

\displaystyle\boxed{{\bm{\tau}}={\tau}K_{AA}^{\vee}-I_{AB}DI_{BA}^{\vee}\,,\qquad{\tau}\in{\mathbb{C}}.}

(34)

{\bm{\tau}}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
iff
{\bm{\tau}}
is symmetric, which is automatic for all
{\tau}
from (
34
), and if
\mathrm{Im}{\bm{\tau}}>0
.
This latter condition follows if
{\tau}
is restricted to the upper half-plane,
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}{\tau}>0,

(35)

because
K_{AA}
is positive definite since the Killing form on
{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
from which it is derived is.
We will eventually identify
{\tau}
with the exactly marginal gauge coupling of the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory.

We refer to the whole 1-dimensional family of SK structures associated to
S
, parameterized by
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
, as an
SK structure orbit
.
Note that for any
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
,
(S_{M},{\bm{\tau}}_{M})\doteq(MSM^{-1},M\circ{\bm{\tau}})
is an equivalent SK structure, and
S
and
S^{\prime}
define the same SK structure orbit.
These equivalences are just the low energy EM duality frame equivalences, which identify Fix
(S)
and Fix
(MSM^{-1})
in the Siegel half-space.
But if an
M\neq 1
exists such that
MSM^{-1}=S
then it implies an identification
{\bm{\tau}}\equiv M\circ{\bm{\tau}}
on
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
, and thus an identification on
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
.
We call its set of inequivalent
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
the
conformal manifold
of the SK structure orbit represented by
S_{(A,B;D)}
.
The group of such identifications on
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
form the
self-duality group of the SK structure orbit
, since the conformal manifold of the SK structure orbit is the quotient of the covering space,
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
, by the action of this group on
{\tau}
.

2.5
Classifying bindings by Ext groups

We now turn to
Step 3
of the method outlined in section
2.2
, which is the first step in determining when two
S_{(A,B;D)}
are integrally equivalent.
This is to determine for given
A,B\in\mathcal{I}
which bindings
D
give integrally inequivalent symplectic representations
S_{(A,B;D)}
.
A binding can be thought of as specifying an extension of the
R_{A}
representation (module) by
R_{B}^{\vee}
,

\displaystyle 1\to R_{B}^{\vee}\to S_{(A,B;D)}\to R_{A}\to 1.

(36)

Such extensions are classified by Ext groups.
In particular, introduce the notion of
inner binding
as a binding of the form (
29
) but with
I_{AB}D\in\mathrm{Mat}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
In this case, by (
33
),
S_{(A,B;D)}\cong_{\mathbb{Z}}S_{(AB,0)}
.
Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be the group of bindings (under addition), and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
be the subgroup of inner bindings.
Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}W}(R_{A},R_{B}^{\vee})={\mathcal{B}}/{\mathcal{B}}_{0}

(37)

classifies the inequivalent
S_{(A,B;D)}
extensions.
The largest denominator appearing in a rational entry in
D
is bounded by the order of
W
, thus giving an upper bound on the size of the
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}W}(R_{A},R_{B}^{\vee})
groups; however, for Weyl groups, the denominators are typically much smaller.
8
8

8

For Weyl groups of rank
r
, the largest denominator grows only as
\sim\sqrt{r}
even though the order of the groups grow as
\sim r!
.

These results are reviewed in appendix
B
.

Note that if
D\propto K_{BB}^{\vee}
, then
L\equiv 0
by (
24
) and (
25
).
So the groups
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
can be computed as the groups of rational and integer
I_{AB}D
, respectively, satisfying (
29
), as long as the ideal in
\mathrm{Mat}(r,{\mathbb{Q}})
generated by
K_{BB}^{\vee}
is quotiented out in computing
{\mathcal{B}}
.
Upon clearing denominators, (
29
) for all
w\in W
becomes a set of linear diophantine equations for the numerators and common denominator of entries of
D
.

Practical computation.

Such equations can be solved by any of various efficient algorithms for computing the Hermite normal form of the coefficient matrix.
The solutions depend on the relative divisibility properties of the matrix elements of the
R_{A}
and
R_{B}^{\vee}
representations, and seems complicated in general.
In particular, the solutions for
D
, and thus the
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}W}(R_{A},R_{B}^{\vee})
groups, do not seem to be expressed in terms of the
K
and
I
intertwiners in any simple way.

Instead, we list the results in the fourth column of table
3
in the case where
B=A
, which are the symplectic representations which preserve a principal polarization.
We prove these results for the most intricate case, the
A_{r}
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{su}(r{+}1)
) series of Weyl groups, in section
3.1
.
But we do not actually prove the results listed in table
3
for the
BC_{r}
and
D_{r}
series of Weyl groups; instead, we have reported the pattern found by brute force computation at low ranks.
We implemented these computations for any Weyl group in a Mathematica notebook which is available from the authors upon request.
A proof of the
BC_{r}
and
D_{r}
series results along the lines of that of the
A_{r}
series results of section
3.1
should not be too hard, just lengthy.

In slightly more detail, in order to compute the extension group, we first consider an arbitrary symmetric
r\times r
matrix
D=(D_{\mu\nu})
, with
D_{{\mu}{\nu}}=D_{{\nu}{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.
In order to impose condition (
29
), we compute the
r
matrices
R_{A}(w_{i})D-DR_{A}^{\vee}(w_{i})
, whose entries are
r^{3}
linear forms of the
r(r{+}1)/2
variables
D_{{\mu}{\nu}}
with integer coefficients (as the matrices
R_{A}(w_{i})
have integer entries).
These linear forms can be repackaged as a linear map of lattices
{\mathcal{Y}}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{r(r+1)/2}\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{r^{3}}
, along with a chosen basis (corresponding to the parametrization of
D
).
Hence
{\mathcal{Y}}
can be seen as a
r^{3}\times r(r+1)/2
matrix with integer entries.
Using the Hermite decomposition algorithm, one can find a
unimodular
map
{\mathcal{U}}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{r^{3}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{r^{3}}
(equivalently, an
r^{3}\times r^{3}
matrix with integer entries and determinant 1) such that
{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{Y}}
is
upper triangular
.
It is then straightforward to compute
({\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{Y}})^{-1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{r^{3}})
, as the upper-triangularity reduces the problem to solving
r^{3}
linear equations in
one
variable.
This allows to write down the generic solution to (
29
) as a linear combination

D={\beta}I_{BA}K_{AB}^{\vee}+n_{1}{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}+\dots+n_{s}{\mathfrak{D}}_{s}

(38)

with
{\beta}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and
n_{1},\dots,n_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Using this form, it is then straightforward to compute the group of bindings, that of inner bindings, and thus the quotient.
See section
3.2
for an explicit example of such a computation.

2.6
Integral equivalence of symplectic representations

Once we have found the inequivalent bindings
D
for a choice of integral Weyl reflection representations
A
and
B
which preserve a given
J^{\vee}
, it still remains to determine which
S_{\mathcal{A}}
with
{\mathcal{A}}=(A,B;D)
are integrally equivalent among all the different choices of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Mathematically, this amounts to classifying the following equivalence classes.
We have seen that a pair
(S,{\bm{\tau}})
determines an SK geometry, where
S:W\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
is a faithful representation of the Weyl group
W_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathfrak{g}})
that is
{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivalent to two copies of the reflection representation of
W
, and
{\bm{\tau}}\in\text{Fix}(S)\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
.
And, based on the EM duality of the IR effective action, two such descriptions define the same geometry,
(S,{\bm{\tau}})\simeq(S^{\prime},{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime})
, if there exists an
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that

\displaystyle MS(w)M^{-1}

\displaystyle=S^{\prime}(w)

and

\displaystyle M\circ{\bm{\tau}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall w\in W.

(39)

Actually, this is not the most general notion of equivalence, and below we will correct it by allowing for equivalences between integral symplectic representations
S
and
S^{\prime}
that are intertwined by some
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
after one of them has been composed with a reflection preserving automorphism of the Weyl group.
But, as a first pass, we classify SK geometries with respect to this definition (
39
) of equivalence.
This is
Step 4
of the classification strategy described in section
2.2
.

The key to classifying these equivalence classes is the following technical lemma that allows us to characterize the
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
matrices
M
that satisfy the intertwining condition in (
39
):

Lemma 1.

Any
\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{R}})
matrix that intertwines
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
(
39
) is of the form

\boxed{{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\left({\gamma}\right)\doteq\begin{pmatrix}1&-I_{A_{1}B_{1}}D_{1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\underline{a}}I_{A_{1}A_{2}}&{\underline{b}}K_{A_{1}B_{2}}^{\vee}\\
{\underline{c}}K_{B_{1}A_{2}}&{\underline{d}}I_{B_{1}B_{2}}^{\vee}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&I_{A_{2}B_{2}}D_{2}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}}\,,

(40)

where

\displaystyle{\gamma}\doteq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\underline{a}}&{\underline{b}}\\
{\underline{c}}&{\underline{d}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\,,

(41)

and

\displaystyle\det\left[{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\left({\gamma}\right)\right]=(\det{\gamma})^{r}\det I_{A_{2}A_{1}}\det I_{B_{1}B_{2}}\,.

(42)

Here
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}=(A_{i},B_{i};D_{i})
as usual, and we underline
{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}},{\underline{c}}
, and
{\underline{d}}
only because we are running out of letters.
The expression (
40
) comes from a direct computation and several uses of Schur’s lemma.
The determinant comes from the formula for a block matrix determinant, and from the identity

K_{B_{2}A_{1}}I_{A_{1}A_{2}}K_{A_{2}B_{1}}^{\vee}=I_{B_{2}B_{1}}^{\vee}
,
which follows from (
26
) and (
27
).

The
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
so defined are thus maps

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}

\displaystyle:\mathrm{GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\to\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{R}}),

(43)

whose image intertwines
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
,

\displaystyle S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}(w)\,{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})\,S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}(w),\qquad\forall w\in W,\ {\gamma}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}}).

(44)

These maps also obey the important composition property:

Lemma 2.

For any
{\gamma},{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and any
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}}\in{\mathfrak{I}}
,

{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}}({\gamma}){\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{C}}}({\gamma}^{\prime})={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{C}}}({\gamma}{\gamma}^{\prime})\,.

(45)

This follows from the identities (
27
).

Such matrices give an equivalence between the two symplectic representations if
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
is integral and has determinant
\pm 1
.
By virtue of (
44
), they satisfy
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}^{t}J^{\vee}_{1}{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\propto J^{\vee}_{2}
, where
J_{i}
are the symplectic forms preserved by the
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i}}
representations.
If
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
have different invariant factors then there does not exist an integral invertible
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
intertwining them.
Thus there can only be an equivalence between representations with equivalent symplectic forms.
Furthermore, if
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
are equivalent symplectic forms, then any
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
intertwining them has determinant
+1
.
So we introduce the sets of
2\times 2
matrices

{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\doteq\left\{\,{\gamma}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\ |\ {\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})\in\mathrm{SL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\,\right\}\,,

(46)

that yield such integral intertwiners.
Note that in general the composition property (
45
) does not require
{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
to form a group.
The determinant condition can only have a solution if
(\det I_{A_{2}A_{1}}\det I_{B_{1}B_{2}})^{1/r}
is rational.
Since by (
32
) the normalized integral pairings preserved by the two representations are
{\bf j}_{1}=I_{A_{1}B_{1}}/\mathrm{gcd}(I_{A_{1}B_{1}})
and
{\bf j}_{2}=I_{A_{2}B_{2}}/\mathrm{gcd}(I_{A_{2}B_{2}})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\det I_{A_{2}A_{1}}\det I_{B_{1}B_{2}}=\det I_{A_{2}B_{2}}\det I_{B_{1}A_{1}}

\displaystyle=(\det{\bf j}_{2}/\det{\bf j}_{1})\left[\mathrm{gcd}(I_{A_{1}B_{1}})/\mathrm{gcd}(I_{A_{2}B_{2}})\right]^{r},

where we have used that
I_{B_{2}A_{2}}I_{A_{2}A_{1}}=I_{B_{2}B_{1}}I_{B_{1}A_{1}}
by (
27
).

{\bf j}_{1}
and
{\bf j}_{2}
are equivalent iff they have the same invariant factors, in which case their determinants are the same.
So, a refinement of
Lemma 1
is

Lemma 3.

Let
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
be symplectic representations with respect to equivalent symplectic forms. Then they are
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalent representations iff there is an
integral

{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})
of the form (
40
) with
{\gamma}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\det{\gamma}=\mathrm{gcd}(I_{A_{2}B_{2}})/\mathrm{gcd}(I_{A_{1}B_{1}})
.

This gives a computable condition for the pair of symplectic representations
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
to be
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalent.
For, since we are assuming that the
I
’s,
K
’s, and
D
’s are given (because we have solved the extension problem described in the previous subsection), integrality of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{1}{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}({\gamma})
amounts to some divisibility criteria on
{\underline{a}}
,
{\underline{b}}
,
{\underline{c}}
, and
{\underline{d}}
which can be solved algorithmically as for the Ext problem.

We have carried out these calculations for all pairs of representations symplectic with respect to a principally polarized symplectic form.
As we discuss in section
2.8
, these are the representations
S_{\mathcal{A}}
with
{\mathcal{A}}=(A,A;D)
.
The results are recorded in the 2nd column of table
1
which lists the
{\mathcal{A}}
equivalence classes in terms of the naming system for the invariant lattices and Ext group elements summarized in table
3
.
The identifications of
{\mathcal{A}}
equivalence classes indicated by blue arrows in that table do not follow from the above lemma.
We will discuss these additional equivalences shortly.
These computations have also been implemented in a Mathematica notebook available from the authors on request.

2.7
SK structure orbit self-duality groups

We now turn to
Step 5
of the method described in section
2.2
, where we characterize the group of self-equivalences of an SK structure.

For
{\mathcal{A}}=(A,B;D)
, the self-intertwiners
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}
take the form,

{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}\left({\gamma}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}1&-I_{AB}D\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\underline{a}}&{\underline{b}}K_{AB}^{\vee}\\
{\underline{c}}K_{BA}&{\underline{d}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&I_{AB}D\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,

(47)

where, as above,
{\gamma}\doteq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\underline{a}}&{\underline{b}}\\
{\underline{c}}&{\underline{d}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.
From
Lemma 2
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}
is a group morphism; in fact, it is an isomorphism onto its image.
We introduce the group
{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
of
2\times 2
real determinant 1 matrices which parameterize the self-intertwiners of the SK structure orbit
{\mathcal{A}}
.
It is therefore defined as

{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}\doteq{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}^{-1}(\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}))\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}}),

(48)

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}^{-1}
denotes the preimage in
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.
It is some discrete subgroup defined by divisibility conditions.
If there were no additional automorphism-twisted intertwiners, then
{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
would be the duality group of the SK orbit
{\mathcal{A}}
; we will refine the definition of S-duality group below when we incorporate twisted intertwiners.

\widetilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}\doteq{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}({\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}})
is thus the group of intertwiners of the
{\mathcal{A}}
SK structure orbit.
9
9

9

It is only a subgroup of the
\widetilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
defined in (
7
) since it does not (yet) contain the automorphism twisted intertwiners.

\widetilde{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
acts on
{\bm{\tau}}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
via the action
{\bm{\tau}}\mapsto{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}({\gamma})\circ{\bm{\tau}}
given in (
A.1
).
If this
{\bm{\tau}}
is in
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
then a calculation shows that this maps the
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
coordinate on
\mathrm{Fix}(S)
via fractional linear transformations,

\displaystyle{\gamma}:{\tau}\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\underline{a}}&{\underline{b}}\\
{\underline{c}}&{\underline{d}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\circ{\tau}=\frac{{\underline{a}}{\tau}+{\underline{b}}}{{\underline{c}}{\tau}+{\underline{d}}}.

(49)

Thus
{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
acts as a group of identifications of the coupling
{\tau}
in the upper half-plane, giving a conformal manifold of the SK structure orbit
{\mathcal{A}}
as
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}/{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
, the modular curve of
{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
10
10

10

Again, upon including automorphism-twisted identifications, as we do below, the S-duality group may be enlarged, and the conformal manifold correspondingly reduced.

(More properly,
{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathcal{A}}
should be defined as a subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
because there is no difference between the action of
{\gamma},-{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
on
{\tau}
; for convenience, we stick with the realization of
{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
as
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
matrices where this
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
identification is understood.)

In the special case where the binding vanishes,
D=0
, we can be even more specific about what the group
{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
is.
In this case
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}}({\gamma})\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
iff

\displaystyle{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\underline{a}}&{\underline{b}}\\
{\underline{c}}&{\underline{d}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{\underline{{\alpha}}}&{\underline{{\beta}}}/\mathrm{gcd}(K_{AB}^{\vee})\\
{\underline{{\gamma}}}/\mathrm{gcd}(K_{AB})&{\underline{{\delta}}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{Q}}),

\displaystyle{\underline{{\alpha}}},{\underline{{\beta}}},{\underline{{\gamma}}},{\underline{{\delta}}}

\displaystyle\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

(50)

By conjugating
{\gamma}\mapsto N{\gamma}N^{-1}
by
N=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sqrt{\mathrm{gcd}(K^{\vee}_{AB})}&0\\
0&1/\sqrt{\mathrm{gcd}(K^{\vee}_{AB})}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, we see that the self-duality group of the
{\mathcal{A}}=(AB,0)
orbit is equivalent to the modular congruence group

\displaystyle{\mathscr{S}}_{(AB,0)}

\displaystyle\simeq{\Gamma}_{0}(n_{AB})\doteq\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\underline{a}}&{\underline{b}}\\
{\underline{c}}&{\underline{d}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}}),\ {\underline{c}}=0\ ({\rm mod}\ n_{AB})\right\},

(51)

where
n_{AB}
is the integer invariant defined in (
28
).

Interlude on outer automorphisms of Weyl groups.

We now turn to including automorphism twisted intertwiners in the SK structure orbit self-duality groups.
First, we summarize a few facts about automorphisms of Weyl groups, mostly taken from

bannai1969automorphisms
;
franzsen2001automorphisms
.
For each Weyl group
W
, one can define:

\blacktriangleright

\mathrm{Aut}(W)
is the group of all group automorphisms of
W
.

\blacktriangleright

\mathrm{Inn}(W)
is the group of inner automorphisms, i.e., those which act on
W
by conjugation,
W\mapsto vWv^{-1}
for some
v\in W
.

\blacktriangleright

\mathrm{Out}(W)=\frac{\mathrm{Aut}(W)}{\mathrm{Inn}(W)}
is the group of outer automorphisms.
They are listed in table
4
.

\blacktriangleright

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
is the group of all
\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}(W)
preserving reflections, i.e., such that
\phi(w)
is a reflection whenever
w
is a reflection. All inner automorphisms are reflection automorphisms,
\mathrm{Inn}(W)\subset\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
.

\blacktriangleright

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)=\frac{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)}{\mathrm{Inn}(W)}
is the group of outer automorphisms preserving reflections, or
reflection outer automorphisms
for short, also listed in table
4
.

In the next paragraph, we will see that in the computation of the modifications of SK structure orbits and their self-duality groups that come from the inclusion of automorphism intertwiners, the relevant group is
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
.

Symmetries of the Coxeter diagram
11
11

11

Recall that Coxeter diagrams of Weyl groups are undirected versions of the Dynkin diagrams of the corresponding Lie algebras, i.e., without the arrow or marking differentiating long from short roots.
for
W
correspond to reflection automorphisms of
W
, since these symmetries interchange nodes of the diagrams which corresponds to interchanging the corresponding simple generating reflections which define
W
as a finite reflection group.
These diagram symmetries are shown in table
4
.
Though they are reflection automorphisms, they are not necessarily outer automorphisms: only the diagram automorphisms of the
BC_{2}
,
D_{2r}
,
F_{4}
, and
G_{2}
Weyl groups are outer.
These turn out to be all the reflection outer automorphisms of Weyl groups.

Finally, as a quotient of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
may fail to be a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
.
But in the Weyl group case, the quotient splits as
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)=\mathrm{Inn}(W)\rtimes\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
, so we can (and do) realize
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)\subset\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
.

W

\mathrm{Out}(W)=\frac{\mathrm{Aut}(W)}{\mathrm{Inn}(W)}

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)=\frac{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)}{\mathrm{Inn}(W)}
