Table 6:
The first 3 columns summarize the S-duality orbit and group predictions from field theory
Aharony:2013hda
;
Bergman:2022otk
.
The red entries highlight the places where these predictions disagree with our classification of SK structures given in table
1
.
The
\pm
signs in the subscripts for the
\mathrm{SO}(N)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
global structures are correlated; thus, for example,
(\mathrm{SO}(N)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{\begin{smallmatrix}\pm+\\[-1.5pt]
+\mp\end{smallmatrix}}
denotes two global structures, not four.
The last column gives the index of the S-duality group as a subgroup of the Hecke group
H_{q}
where
q=3,4,6
is determined (
192
) by the lacing number,
\ell=1,2,3
, respectively, of the gauge algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
It counts the number of global structures in each orbit.
Note that
H_{3}=\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

A comparison of this table with our results for SK structures given in table
1
shows broad agreement between the S-duality groups of SK structure orbits and of global structure orbits, and a looser qualitative agreement between our listing of SK structure representations and global structures of gauge groups.
Indicated in red in both tables are the entries where either S-duality groups do not agree, or where the counting of the number of orbits do not agree.

In this section we discuss these discrepancies and argue that

1.

The disagreement of the S-duality groups for certain orbits in the
BC_{r}
series (including
A_{1}
) is an “IR accident”: the SK structure is a low energy observable which, in these cases, simply fails to distinguish between inequivalent field theories.
Indeed, it is a striking and unexpected fact that in so many cases the SK structure successfully distinguishes
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories, whereas there are many other examples of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
theories where the scale invariant CB geometry fails to distinguish them.

2.

The counting of orbits disagreement in the
D_{4k}
cases, as well as the disagreement of the S-duality group for an orbit of the
D_{4k+2}
cases, are due to an error in the field theory literature: the SK structure computation gives the correct answer.

3.

Finally, the pattern observed that, in general, the number of field theory global structures in a given orbit is greater than the number of SK structure representations in the corresponding orbit, is to be expected, and does not indicate a physical contradiction.
Instead, only a correspondence between equivalence classes (orbits) of SK structures and S-duality orbits of global structures is expected.

Before addressing these three points, we pause to emphasize the logic of comparing low energy effective actions on the moduli space of vacua (SK structures) to S-duality conjectures in the field theory.
Field theory S-duality conjectures the exact equivalence between CFTs described by various weak coupling limits of their exactly marginal coupling.
These conjectures are supported, of course, by an overwhelming amount of evidence by now, but have not been proven in field theory.
A way of testing these conjectures is to compute all inequivalent moduli space geometries consistent with unbroken
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
supersymmetry without making assumptions about S-duality properties or about the spectrum of massive BPS states out on the moduli space.
But mismatches between moduli space geometries and field theories can occur for two reasons:

\blacktriangleright

moduli space geometries fail to distinguish inequivalent field theories, or

\blacktriangleright

moduli space geometries exist which do not arise from any (known) field theory.

We limit the second possibility by putting a few additional constraints on the geometries.
These are:

(i)

Do not allow complex singularities on a CB slice of the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
moduli space.

(ii)

Assume the existence of at least one free field theory point with a simple gauge group on the conformal manifold of the structure.

(iii)

Restrict to principally polarized SK structures.

These then restrict to the Weyl group orbifolds studied in this paper, as explained in appendix
A
.

Constraint (i) is imposed since it is known that all
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories have such CBs.
Though there also exist CBs of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories with complex singularities
Bourget:2018ond
;
Argyres:2018wxu
, they all arise from gauging certain discrete 0-form symmetries of theories without such singularities.
This constraint eliminates these geometries as well as other possible “exotic” singular geometries compatible with
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
supersymmetry, but possessing no known field theory origin.

Constraint (ii) is imposed since all
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories have an exactly marginal coupling with, by definition, a weak coupling limit.
From the discussion in appendix
A
, the Kähler structure of an SK geometry which satisfies the constraint (i) and which has a weak coupling point on its conformal manifold where it is described by a sYM lagrangian with simple gauge group
{\mathfrak{g}}
, will be that of a Weyl group orbifold.
By adopting this constraint we are eliminating geometries that could correspond to possible “exotic”
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
SCFTs which have no weak coupling limits
Papadodimas_2010
.

The third and final restriction, (iii), derives from wanting to compare to absolute
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
theories.
Absolute
4d
gauge theories, such as
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories, are defined by a choice of maximal mutually local probe lines
Gaiotto:2010be
;
Aharony:2013hda
, meaning that the Dirac pairing between them is principal.
14
14

14

This global data is often referred to as the global structure or global variant of the gauge theory.

This choice of probe lines in the sYM SCFT corresponds to a choice of probe lines that renders the low-energy
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
gauge theory at a generic point on the CB to be absolute.
Such abelian probe lines are specified by a charge lattice that is maximal and mutually local with respect to the Dirac pairing and contains the physical BPS charge lattice of the sYM theory
DelZotto:2022ras
;
Argyres:2022kon
.
A lattice of probe lines with its principal Dirac pairing is referred to as a
line lattices
in
Argyres:2022kon
, and it’s identified with the homology lattice and symplectic pairing, respectively, of the principally polarized abelian variety fiber of a (necessarily) principal SK structure.
It is for this reason that we focus on SK structures with principal polarization.

In section
5
we discuss some possible consequences of lifting constraint (iii) as well as of allowing non-simple
{\mathfrak{g}}
in constraint (ii).

4.1
The
BC_{r}
disagreement

In the
A_{1}=BC_{1}
case, though there is a single SK structure orbit and a single S-duality orbit of
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{su}(2)
global structures, the S-duality groups of the two orbits do not match.
The SK orbit duality group is
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
while the field theory S-duality group is
{\Gamma}_{0}(2)
, an index 3 subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Correspondingly, the field theory conformal manifold is a 3-fold cover of that of the SK orbit.
This is not a contradiction: the CB geometry, after all, is just some observable of the field theory, and there is no reason it need uniquely characterize the field theory, so may fail to distinguish inequivalent field theories.

Conversely, the SK structure result cannot be interpreted as a mistake in the field theory S-duality conjectures.
For there are three weak coupling global structures,
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
\mathrm{SO}(3)_{\pm}
in the notation of
Aharony:2013hda
, and two of them, namely
\mathrm{SO}(3)_{\pm}
, are continuously connected at weak coupling in
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM.
Thus there can only be either: a single S-duality orbit containing all three weak coupling limits (which is the usual S-duality conjecture); or two S-duality orbits with one containing the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
limit and the other containing the
\mathrm{SO}(3)_{\pm}
limits.
The SK structure result cannot distinguish between these two since it identifies the SK geometries of all three weak coupling limits.

The S-duality group discrepancies for the
BC_{r>1}
theories are similar.
In these cases there is an SK structure which fails to distinguish between two global structures,
\mathrm{Sp}(N)
and
\mathrm{SO}(N+1)_{+}
, instead of three as in the
A_{1}
case.
15
15

15

In the
BC_{2}
case
\mathfrak{sp}(4)=\mathfrak{so}(5)
so global structure
\mathrm{Sp}(4)=\mathrm{Spin}(5)
which is still distinct from
\mathrm{SO}(5)_{+}
.

The SK orbit duality group is once again a 3-fold cover of the field theory S-duality group.
The fact that this 3-fold cover fails to distinguish only 2 weak coupling limits indicates that it actually makes a 2-fold self-identification of one of the weak coupling limits.
In this case it is the
\mathrm{SO}(N+1)_{+}
limit, where the SK structure cannot distinguish between gauge couplings
{\tau}
and
{\tau}+1
in the normalization where the theta angle periodicity is
{\tau}\sim{\tau}+2
.
This purely weak coupling discrepancy makes it clear that the SK orbit result is simply an “IR accident” and cannot be taken as an indication of a mistake in the field theory S-duality conjecture.

In more detail, S-duality transformations of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories form subgroups of the level-
q
Hecke groups
H_{q}
for
q=3,4,6
corresponding to the lacing number for the gauge Lie algebra
\ell=1,2,3
, respectively
Dorey:1996hx
.
As a reminder, the lacing number is
\ell=1
for simply-laced
{\mathfrak{g}}
’s,
\ell=2
for
BC_{r}
and
F_{4}
, and
\ell=3
for
G_{2}
.
Also,
H_{3}=\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, but
H_{4}
and
H_{6}
are inequivalent discrete subgroups of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
; they are defined in appendix
C
.
The maximal possible field theory S-duality group,
H_{q}
, would be one that fixes the gauge algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
But precisely for the
BC_{r>2}
cases, GNO duality
Goddard:1976qe
relates two different Lie algebras,
\mathfrak{sp}(2N)
and
\mathfrak{so}(2N+1)
.
So in these cases the maximal S-duality group is only the index-2 subgroup of
H_{4}

Argyres:2006qr
, which happens to be isomorphic to
{\Gamma}_{0}(2)
, which is an index-3 subgroup of
H_{3}=\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
as reviewed in appendix
C
.
So the fact that the
H_{4}
group is not a subgroup of the SK orbit duality group,
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, is not a contradiction since the predicted
{\Gamma}_{0}(2)
S-duality group is a subgroup.

These failures of the SK structure result to distinguish between inequivalent field theories raises the question of what other observables, beyond the SK structure of the moduli space, could faithfully distinguish them.
Since this failure is detected even at weak coupling, such additional observables are easy to provide.
One, described in
Gaiotto:2010be
;
Aharony:2013hda
;
Argyres:2022kon
, is the lattice of EM charges of (finite energy) BPS states in the field theory as a sublattice of the probe line charge lattice.
This clearly distinguishes the weak coupling limits since it is used as the definition of the gauge theory global structure.
There are hints, coming from studies of rank-1
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
geometries
Caorsi:2018ahl
;
Closset:2021lhd
;
Closset:2023pmc
, that the BPS charge sublattice of the probe line lattice can be detected from some subtle arithmetic properties of the SK structures we have presented.
We will comment on these in the context of non-principally polarized SK structures in section
5
.

A more speculative proposal for a moduli space observable which could distinguish field theory global structures are the SK structures of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
supersymmetry-preserving mass deformed moduli spaces.
The evidence that this might be a rich enough observable comes mostly from the study of rank-1 CB geometries
Argyres:2015ffa
;
Argyres:2015gha
where the 3-fold degeneracy of the (principally polarized) scale invariant
A_{1}
SK structure is lifted, as are many similar degeneracies in other rank-1
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
geometries.

Finally, there is a disagreement of our SK structure results with a claim in
Argyres:2023eij
, who, in their study of rank-2
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
Seiberg-Witten curves, claim that the two SW curve orbits for
BC_{2}
have duality groups which are both
{\Gamma}_{0}(2)
.
This is an index-2 subgroup of the
H_{4}
duality group of the
\{({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},1),({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{\vee},1)\}
SK orbit reported in table
1
, and a 3-fold cover of the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
duality group of the
\{({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},0),({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{\vee},0)\}
SK orbit reported there.
(Comparing to the field theory predictions of table
6
, they disagree with the
\{\mathrm{SO}(5)_{-}\}
orbit, but agree with the
\{\mathrm{Sp}(4),\mathrm{SO}(5)_{+}\}
orbit: the opposite of what we have computed here.)
Both claims of
Argyres:2023eij
are mistaken; in the case of the curve for the first orbit it is due to missing the isometry of the CB resulting from the
BC_{2}
reflection outer automorphism, and in the case of the curve for the second orbit it is due to missing a set of identifications hidden by the particular algebraic form of the Seiberg-Witten curve that they used.

4.2
The
D_{2k}
disagreement

The automorphism-twisted equivalences of SK structures are crucial in obtaining the Hecke group S-duality groups that appear in the
BC_{2}
,
G_{2}
and
F_{4}
non-simply laced cases, and this was illustrated in detail in the
BC_{2}
case in section
3.3
.
But in the
D_{2k}
Weyl groups also have a non-trivial reflection outer automorphism, and including their associated equivalences leads to a modification of the counting of global structures relative to that given in the field theory literature, and also to a modification of the S-duality group of one orbit of the
D_{4k+2}
theories relative to the field theory prediction.
This modification of the S-duality group in the case of
D_{6}
was also illustrated in detail in section
3.2
.

While one could interpret this mismatch between our geometric classification and the field theory S-duality conjectures as another instance of the geometry failing to distinguish between inequivalent field theories, in this case it turns out the geometrical classification is correct, and a mistake — a double counting of certain field theories — was made in the field theory literature.
The reason is very simple.
They Weyl group reflection outer automorphism in the
D_{2k}
cases, unlike in the non-simply-laced cases, lifts to a gauge algebra outer automorphism.
In particular, it is the automorphism which interchanges the spinor and conjugate spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
.
This symmetry means that at the level of the sYM gauge theory lagrangian there is no way of distinguishing, even in principle, between an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory in which we put probe lines charged in the spinor representation or in the conjugate spinor representation: they differ just by the arbitrary choice of a name.
In the
\mathfrak{so}(8k)
theories this just means that the pairs of orbits in table
6
with
\mathrm{Ss}(N)_{\pm}
and
\mathrm{Sc}(N)_{\pm}
global structures (shown in red) are indistinguishable, so two of the S-duality orbits should be removed from the list.
In the
\mathfrak{so}(8k+4)
theories the
\mathrm{Ss}(N)_{\pm}
and
\mathrm{Sc}(N)_{\pm}
global structures appear in the same orbit, so their equivalence implies a 2-fold identification of its conformal manifold, and so a 2-fold enlargement of its S-duality group from
{\Gamma}_{0}(4)
(shown in red in table
6
) to
{\Gamma}_{0}(2)
.

One might object that once we have a theory with, say, semi-simple gauge algebra
\mathfrak{so}(8k)\times\mathfrak{so}(8k)
then though the overall distinction between spinor and conjugate spinor representations is a matter of convention, the
relative
distinction (i.e., whether they are the same or opposite) between the two spinor representations in each factor is observable.
This is incorrect because for
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories the two simple gauge factors of the theory are decoupled, and so there is no observable that can distinguish between a (spinor
\times
spinor) and a (spinor
\times
conjugate spinor) theory.
If, on the other hand, there exist semi-simple versions of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
theories whose SK geometry does not factorize because of some twisting of their abelian variety fibers, then in these theories the relative spinor versus conjugate spinor distinction may be observable.
The possibility of such twisted geometries is discussed further in section
5
.

D_{4}
is a special case where the reflection outer automorphism group is enlarged to
S_{3}
.
However, these automorphisms, permuting the
S
,
C
, and
V
representations as well as some of the bindings of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}
or root lattice representations, always act among the untwisted orbits, so only identify them without enlarging their self-duality groups, as shown in table
1
.
This symmetry makes the
D_{4}
global structure orbits in table
6
with
\mathrm{SO}(8)_{\pm}
,
\mathrm{Ss}(8)_{\pm}
, and
\mathrm{Sc}(8)_{\pm}
indistinguishable.

4.3
SK structures versus global structures of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
sYM gauge theory.

A result of the discussion so far in this section is that S-duality orbits of sYM global structures are in 1-to-1 correspondence to the orbits of CB SK structures under EM duality.
One may wonder whether there is a canonical way of matching subsets of SK structures to the individual global structures that make up their S-duality orbits.
Comparing our SK structure representations
{\mathcal{A}}=(R_{A},D)
shown in the second column of table
1
to the global forms of the sYM gauge group shown in the second column of table
6
, we can see that, heuristically, different choices of the representation
R_{A}
can be identified with the global form of the gauge group, while the binding matrix Ext class
D
plays the role of the “discrete theta angles”.
But these identifications are not unique, and in many cases there are more global structures than SK representations.

On the one hand, this mismatch is not physically significant, and reflects the fact that gauge invariance is merely a redundancy in the description of the field theory.
On the field theory side different gauge group global structures are related by S-duality equivalences, while on the SK geometry side, different constructions of
(R_{A},D)
symplectic representations are related by physically unobservable basis changes (integral equivalences).
Another way of saying this is that a division of an S-duality orbit into global structures corresponds to a tiling of their conformal manifolds by copies of
H_{q}
fundamental domains in
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
.
There are infinitely many arbitrary choices that can be made in such a tiling.
As a simple example, even in a weak coupling limit, if
w
copies of
H_{q}
fundamental domains have a common weak coupling cusp on the conformal manifold corresponding to an enlarged
{\theta}\sim{\theta}+2\pi w
theta angle periodicity there, there is a continuous family of tilings by
w
intervals in
{\theta}
of width
2\pi
as
{\theta}\in[{\theta}_{0}+2\pi n,{\theta}_{0}+2\pi(n{+}1)]
parameterized by
{\theta}_{0}
.

On the other hand, at a weak coupling limit, the global form of the gauge group is a physical property of the gauge theory that can be observed on the CB as a relation between the charge lattice of probe lines and its charge sublattice of BPS states
Gaiotto:2010be
;
Aharony:2013hda
;
Argyres:2022kon
.
This does not uniquely specify the associated discrete theta angles, but does pick out the global form of the gauge group.
At a weak coupling point on the conformal manifold one relates the global form of the gauge group to the integral representation of the Weyl group, since they are both related to choices of subgroups of the center of the simply connected gauge group, as described in section
2.3
.
This explains the qualitative matching between global structures and symplectic Weyl representations within the orbits listed in tables
1
and
6
.

Furthermore, the counting of the number of global structures in each orbit is also an invariant observable.
Since the conformal manifolds described above are the fundamental domains of finite-index subgroups of the Hecke groups
H_{q}
in the
{\tau}
upper half-space, they are (well-studied)
modular curves
.
These are Riemann surfaces with marked points of three types:
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
orbifold points,
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
orbifold points, and cusps.
Physically the orbifold points (a.k.a., elliptic points) correspond to couplings where the 0-form symmetry group is enhanced by the orbifold group, while the cusps correspond to weak coupling limits.
Each cusp also has a positive integer,
w
, the
width
, associated to it.
Physically, the width at a cusp corresponds to the
{\theta}
angle periodicity
{\theta}\sim{\theta}+2\pi w
at that weak coupling point in the standard normalization.
The geometry of the conformal manifold (modular curve) is described by the number and types of the orbifold points, the number and widths of the cusps, and the genus of the curve.
These are computed for the modular curves of many modular congruence subgroups; see e.g.,
diamond2006first
.
The sum of all the widths is the index of the modular subgroup in
H_{q}
which counts the number of global structures in that S-duality orbit.
This number is recorded for the field results of
Aharony:2013hda
in the 4th column of table
6
.
(As discussed above, this has one incorrect entry for the
D_{2k+1}
case, shown in red, which should be 3 instead of 6.)

Nevertheless, although there is no
canonical
way of relating symplectic representations of the Weyl group to global structures, we can still make such a correspondence “by hand”.
We illustrate this for
\mathfrak{su}(N)
; similar identifications can be made for the other simply-laced algebras, but it does not work in as simple a way for the non-simply-laced algebras, where there is no longer a simple correspondence between representations and central subgroups, as mentioned in section
2.3
.
This illustration will also serve to clarify the arbitrary choices that are made in making such a correspondence.

For
\mathfrak{su}(N)
the lattices are labeled by divisors
d|N
, with
d=N
the root lattice and
d=1
the weight lattice, corresponding to the central groups
H={\mathbb{Z}}_{d}
; see table
3
.
Let’s associate symplectic representations to gauge group global forms by the rule

\displaystyle S_{(d,z)}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow(\mathrm{SU}(N)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{N/d})_{z},

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle|N,

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{d},

(116)

where the
z
subscript on the right is the “discrete theta angle” introduced in
Aharony:2013hda
.
Note that we are labeling the representations by divisors
d
and by binding index
z
mod
d
, even though we saw in section
3.1
that most of these representations are, in fact, integrally equivalent.

Why did we make the “wrong” correspondence,
S_{(d,0)}\leftrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{N/d}
, which inverts
d
?
For no good (i.e., gauge invariant) reason: we could have as easily made instead a
S_{(d,0)}\leftrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{d}
correspondence, or even more complicated ones.
Another way of thinking about this is that the correspondence (
116
) is the “natural” one for the GNO dual “magnetic” gauge algebra instead of the “electric” gauge algebra.
All this is just to emphasize that the following correspondence to global forms is not in any way canonical.

With the choice (
116
), the
S
and
T
generators of the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
group act on the
(d,z)
representation labels as
Bergman:2022otk

T

\displaystyle:(d,z)\mapsto(d^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\left(d,z+\frac{N}{d}\right),

\displaystyle z^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{d^{\prime}},

(117)

S

\displaystyle:(d,z)\mapsto(d^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\left(\frac{N}{\mathrm{gcd}(d,z)},\frac{N\,(d-z)}{d\,\mathrm{gcd}(d,z)}\right),

\displaystyle z^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{d^{\prime}},

where in the
(d^{\prime},z^{\prime})
’s on the right sides,
z^{\prime}
is defined modulo
d^{\prime}
.
Indeed, it is possible to show that these actions on
(d,z)
obey
\textsf{S}^{2}=(\textsf{S}\textsf{T})^{3}=1
.
Thus
S
and
T
generate
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

We claim these
S
and
T
maps correspond to integral equivalences of the
S_{(d,z)}
symplectic representations.
It is complicated to write the explicit forms of the
M({\gamma})
in (
65
) intertwining
S_{(d,z)}
with
S_{(d^{\prime},z^{\prime})}
for the
T
and
S
actions defined in (
117
).
However, it is easy to show that such equivalences must exist, using the result of section
3.1
that
S_{(d,z)}\cong S_{(\mathrm{gcd}(s_{d},z),0)}
.
Indeed it is a short calculation to show that
\mathrm{gcd}(s_{d^{\prime}},z^{\prime})=\mathrm{gcd}(s_{d},z)
for the
(d^{\prime},z^{\prime})
on the right side of (
117
), and thus that they are equivalent representations.

The S-duality orbits of
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{su}(4)

in (
117
) acting on the symplectic representations
S_{(d,z)}
associated to the global forms by (
116
).

For a concrete example, for
\mathfrak{su}(4)
this set of representations are acted on by
S
and
T
as in figure
1
.
This gives the two S-duality orbits and the action of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on the global structures within each orbit, and reproduces the S-duality orbit diagram familiar from
Aharony:2013hda
.

5
Non-principally polarized SK geometries and twisted products

In addition to the principally polarized geometries we have discussed so far, there are also geometries whose Dirac pairing
J
has invariant factors other than 1.
These have the interpretation as corresponding to
relative
QFTs
Freed:2012bs
, i.e., field theories which are the boundary of a non-invertible topological field theory.
The low energy physics on the CB reflects the absolute versus relative distinction as follows. The Dirac pairing on the lattice,
{\Lambda}_{\rm{charge}}
, of charges of finite energy states may be non-principal, and may be refined to a principally polarized “line lattice”,
{\Lambda}_{\rm{line}}\supset{\Lambda}_{\rm{charge}}
, by including a maximal set of genuine probe line operators
Gaiotto:2010be
.
So the CB geometry of the relative theory is that with the
{\Lambda}_{\rm{charge}}
pairing,
J
, so has an SK geometry in which the Weyl group is represented in
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
By contrast, the SK geometry of the CB of an absolute theory is one in which the Weyl group is represented in
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, as discussed in the previous three sections, where we saw that the different S-duality orbits of the global structures of
Aharony:2013hda
correspond to the inequivalent
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
representations of the Weyl group.

The Dirac pairing on
{\Lambda}_{\rm{charge}}
is an observable property of a QFT with a Coulomb vacuum, so not all of the SK geometries we can construct using Weyl group orbifolds are physically realized as the geometries of absolute or relative QFTs.
Using semiclassical techniques, the Dirac pairing on
{\Lambda}_{\rm{charge}}
of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories can be determined, as reviewed in
Argyres:2022kon
.
For instance, the charge lattice Dirac pairing for the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
theory has invariant factors

\displaystyle\text{invariant factors of }J_{\rm{charge}}=(1,\ldots,1,N),

(118)

where there are
N{-}2
“1” entries.

On the other hand we can form symplectic representations
S_{(A,B;D)}
(
31
) with pairing given by (
5
) with (
32
).

Here we chose to normalize the pairing so that the gcd of its invariant factors is 1, i.e., so that its smallest invariant factor is 1.
But since the SK structure does not depend on the overall normalization of the pairing, we can just as well work with
{\bf j}=I^{\vee}_{AB}
, with the understanding that we may have to multiply by an overall factor to clear denominators.

For
\mathfrak{su}(N)
, the possible inequivalent irreducible representations of the Weyl group are labeled by the divisors
d
of
N
, i.e.,
A,B\in\{d\text{ such that }d|N\}
.
The invariant factors of the invariant Dirac pairing on
S_{(A,B;D)}=S_{(dd^{\prime},D)}
are the invariant factors of
I_{dd^{\prime}}
which are

\displaystyle\text{invariant factors of }I_{dd^{\prime}}=(1,\ldots,1,d/d^{\prime})

(119)

as computed from
I_{dd^{\prime}}
given in (
67
).
This shows there is a unique integral symplectic representation of the Weyl group with the physical charge lattice polarization, namely that with
(d,d^{\prime})=(1,N)
.
Indeed, there is a unique
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
representation of the Weyl group with these values of
(d,d^{\prime})
— i.e., the Ext group is trivial, so we can set the binding matrix to
D=0
.
This corresponds to the expectation that there is a unique “maximally relative”
\mathfrak{su}(N)

{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory.
It therefore has a single connected conformal manifold with S-duality group
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
(as opposed to a subgroup).

SK geometries with other values of
d^{\prime}/d
therefore do not correspond to the maximally relative field theory.
Clearly those with
d^{\prime}/d=1
are principally polarized, and correspond to the absolute theories.
If
1<d^{\prime}/d<N
is integral, then these geometries correspond to “intermediate relative” field theories, which are those for which the charge lattice has been refined to a line lattice, but without choosing a
maximal
set of probe line charges.
By contrast, values of
d^{\prime}/d\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
cannot be found in this way.
Since the overall normalization of
J
is not determined by the SK structure, we can clear denominators to make
J
integral with invariant factors to
(1,d,\ldots,d,dd^{\prime})
(assuming
d
and
d^{\prime}
coprime for simplicity).
In general, such Dirac pairings do not occur for any intermediate line lattices.
On the other hand, non-integral
d^{\prime}/d
could occur as the polarizations of lattices of non-mutually-local probe lines, i.e., of lines which are non-genuine line operators, which means lines which are boundaries of topological surface operators.
These make sense as low energy CB
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
gauge theories, but it is difficult to see how they can be defined as sensible kinds of local QFTs relative to a higher-dimensional symmetry TFT.

Similar statements apply to the other simple Lie algebras.

Even if one is only interested in absolute field theories, non-principally polarized SK geometries are still relevant.
For there may be principally polarized geometries for
non-simple
— i.e., product — gauge algebras which are built from non-principally polarized factor geometries.
(Indeed, examples of absolute product field theories which are formed from the product of relative field theory factors are known in the case of 6d (2,0) SCFTs
Gukov:2020btk
.)

Consider two orbifold CB geometries

\displaystyle\text{CB}_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{C}}^{r_{i}}/(W_{i})_{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle=1,2,

(120)

where
W_{i}
are two Weyl groups.
To define these geometries we need to also specify integral symplectic representations

\displaystyle S_{i}:(W_{i})_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}_{i}}(2r_{i},{\mathbb{Z}})

(121)

which leave invariant lattices
{\Lambda}_{i}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}^{2r_{i}}
with invariant symplectic pairings

\displaystyle J_{i}:{\Lambda}_{i}\times{\Lambda}_{i}\to{\mathbb{Z}}.

(122)

We can obviously form the direct product geometry,
\text{CB}_{\times}\doteq\text{CB}_{1}\times\text{CB}_{2}
, where
(W_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}\times(W_{2})_{\mathbb{C}}
acts via the reducible representation
S_{\times}=(S_{1},1)\oplus(1,S_{2})
.
Then the induced invariant symplectic pairing is
J_{\times}=J_{1}\oplus J_{2}
, whose invariant factors are, more or less, the union of those of
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
.
In particular, if either of the
J_{i}
are not principally polarized, then neither is
J_{\times}
.

We raise the question: are there principally-polarized “twisted” product CBs,

\displaystyle\text{CB}_{D}

\displaystyle\doteq\text{CB}_{1}\times_{D}\text{CB}_{2}

(123)

which, when viewed as
Kähler
geometries are product geometries,
\text{CB}_{D}\simeq_{\mathbb{C}}\text{CB}_{\times}
?
In other words, the “twisting” involves only the integral symplectic representation specifying the SK structure.
That means that the integral symplectic representation of the orbifold group,

\displaystyle S_{D}:(W_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}\times(W_{2})_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{Sp}(2(r_{1}{+}r_{2}),{\mathbb{Z}}),

(124)

should be principally polarized (as shown) and should be rationally equivalent to the product representation but not integrally equivalent,

\displaystyle S_{D}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}S_{\times},

but

\displaystyle S_{D}

\displaystyle\not\simeq_{\mathbb{Z}}S_{\times}.

(125)

That is, as should be familiar now and is explained in appendix
B
, over the integers
S_{D}
should be reducible but indecomposable,

\displaystyle S_{D}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}S_{1}(g_{1})&L(g_{1},g_{2})\\
0&S_{2}(g_{2})\end{pmatrix},

with

\displaystyle L(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=S_{1}(g_{1})D-DS_{2}(g_{2})

(126)

with
D
some fixed rational matrix independent of
(g_{1},g_{2})\in W_{1}\times W_{2}
.

Now, a short calculation shows that a symplectic pairing invariant under (
126
) must have the form

\displaystyle J_{D}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\kappa}_{1}J_{1}+{\kappa}_{2}DJ_{2}D^{t}&-{\kappa}_{2}DJ_{2}\\
-{\kappa}_{2}J_{2}D^{t}&{\kappa}_{2}J_{2}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle{\kappa}_{i}

\displaystyle\in{\mathbb{Q}}.

(127)

Assuming there exists a non-vanishing
D
such that
S_{D}
is an integral representation, then there are some minimal value(s) of
{\kappa}_{1,2}
such that
J_{D}
is integral and has smallest invariant factor 1.
The question of the existence of twisted principally polarized representations
S_{D}
is thus equivalent to the existence of a non-vanishing extension
D
and rational numbers
{\kappa}_{i}
such that
J_{D}
in (
127
) is principal, i.e., all its invariant factors are 1.

It follows from (
127
) that

\displaystyle\det J_{D}={\kappa}_{1}^{2r_{1}}{\kappa}_{2}^{2r_{2}}\det J_{1}\det J_{2},

(128)

independent of
D
.
We are interested in the case where
J_{D}
is principally polarized, so
\det J_{D}=1
, while either or both
J_{i}
are not, so
\det J_{1}\det J_{2}>1
and integral.
This, (
128
), rationality of the
{\kappa}_{i}
, and the integrality of
J_{D}
put strong constraints on the possible invariant factors of
J_{i}
.
In general, this is an algebraically complicated question.
We leave the exploration of such “twisted” product
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories to a later work.
But we show now that non-trivial solutions can exist, by demonstrating a simple, familiar, though somewhat degenerate, example.

5.1
\mathfrak{u}(N)

{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM

We now construct the twisted product (
126
) of the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
and
\mathfrak{su}(N)

{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM geometries.

Take
r_{1}=1
and
r_{2}=N-1
with

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle={\epsilon}\otimes{\rm diag}\{N\}=N{\epsilon},

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle={\epsilon}\otimes{\rm diag}\{1,\ldots,1,N\}\doteq{\epsilon}\otimes{\bf j},

(129)

where
{\epsilon}
is the
2\times 2
antisymmetric unit matrix.
16
16

16

Note that if you reverse the roles of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, no solution exists.

Here we have chosen the non-principal pairing,
J_{2}
, of the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
theory to be that of the “maximally relative”
\mathfrak{su}(N)
sYM CB geometry described earlier — i.e., that with symplectic representation
S_{2}\doteq S_{(d,d^{\prime};D)}=S_{(1,N;0)}
.
The normalization of the pairing
J_{1}
is not constrained by physics since the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
theory is free.
We have chosen it to be proportional to
N
so that a solution to (
128
) with square rational but non-integer
{\kappa}_{1}
exists.
We will see below that this allows for potentially non-trivial twistings.

Indeed, (
128
) implies

1=\det J_{D}={\kappa}_{1}^{2}{\kappa}_{2}^{2N-2}N^{4}
so

\displaystyle{\kappa}_{1}=N^{-2}{\kappa}_{2}^{1-N}.

(130)

Integrality of the lower right block of
J_{D}
implies
{\kappa}_{2}
is integral.
Taking
{\kappa}_{2}>1
dramatically increases the denominator of
{\kappa}_{1}
making arranging the integrality of the upper left block of
J_{D}
increasingly difficult, especially if we are looking for a solution which is uniform in
N
.
This leads us to guess that

\displaystyle{\kappa}_{2}

\displaystyle=1,

and so

\displaystyle{\kappa}_{1}

\displaystyle=N^{-2}.

(131)

Now analyze the integrality of the upper right block of
J_{D}
.
Write the binding matrix in terms of
r_{1}\times r_{2}=1\times(N-1)
row vectors as

\displaystyle D\doteq\begin{pmatrix}v&w\\
x&y\end{pmatrix},

(132)

so that
DJ_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}w{\bf j}&-v{\bf j}\\
y{\bf j}&-x{\bf j}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Then, since the first
N-2
diagonal entries of
{\bf j}
are 1, the first
N-2
entries of the row vectors
v
,
w
,
x
, and
y
are all integers.
And since integer entries of
D
can be set to zero by a basis change, we can set the first
N-2
entries of these rows to 0.
Since the last entry in
{\bf j}
is
N
, the last entry of the rows can have denominator
N
.
So set the binding to the
2\times(N-1)
matrix

\displaystyle D\doteq\frac{1}{N}\begin{pmatrix}\vec{0}&{\alpha}&\vec{0}&{\beta}\\
\vec{0}&{\gamma}&\vec{0}&{\delta}\end{pmatrix},

(133)

where the
\vec{0}
’s are
(N-2)
-component zero row vectors, and
{\alpha},{\beta},{\gamma},{\delta}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
.

Now compute the
2\times 2
upper left block in
J_{D}
to find that it is
{\epsilon}(1+{\alpha}{\delta}-{\beta}{\gamma})/N
.
So for it to be integral we must have

\displaystyle{\alpha}{\delta}-{\beta}{\gamma}=-1\ ({\rm mod}\ N).

(134)

Putting (
131
) – (
134
) together gives an integral and principal solution to (
127
).

We now have to check whether there are values of
{\alpha},{\beta},{\gamma},{\delta}
satisfying (
134
) which is a non-trivial binding for the appropriate Weyl
(\mathfrak{u}(1))
and Weyl
(\mathfrak{su}(N))
integral representations.
Weyl
(\mathfrak{u}(1))=1
is trivial, so its only irreducible representation is
{\rho}_{1}(1)=1
, and its rank-2 symplectic representation is just
S_{1}={\rho}_{1}\oplus{\rho}_{1}
, so

\displaystyle S_{1}(1)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(135)

On the other hand, the invariant Dirac pairing,
J_{2}
, of the “maximally relative” symplectic integral representation,
S_{2}
, of Weyl
(\mathfrak{su}(N))
, is given in (
129
), and

\displaystyle S_{2}=S_{(1,N;0)}=\begin{pmatrix}R_{1}^{\vee}&0\\
0&R_{N}\end{pmatrix}.

(136)

Then from (
126
), (
135
), and (
136
), the upper right
2\times 2(N-1)
block of
S_{D}
is

\displaystyle D(1-S_{2})=\frac{1}{N}\begin{pmatrix}{\alpha}(1-R_{1}^{\vee})_{N-1,j}&0\\
0&{\delta}(1-R_{N})_{N-1,j}\end{pmatrix},

(137)

where only the last rows of the
1-R_{1}^{\vee}
and
1-R_{N}
matrices appear because of the form (
133
) of the
D
binding matrix.

Using the explicit form of the
R_{d}
representation matrices computed in the bases used in section
3.1
, we find

\displaystyle(1-R_{1}^{-t}(w_{k}))_{N-1,j}

\displaystyle=-{\delta}_{j,N-2}{\delta}_{k,N-1}

\displaystyle(1-R_{N}(w_{k}))_{N-1,j}

\displaystyle=+{\delta}_{j,1}{\delta}_{k,1}.

(138)

These are not divisible by
N
, so we must have
{\alpha}={\delta}=0
(mod
N
) for
S_{D}
to be integral.
This implies from (
134
) that we must have
{\beta}{\gamma}=1
(mod
N
) if
S_{D}
is to be principally polarized.
Since
{\beta}
and
{\gamma}
do not appear in (
137
), we can consistently choose them to be
{\beta}={\gamma}=1
.

Thus we have constructed a “twisted”
\mathfrak{u}(N)
SK structure suitable for describing an absolute
\mathfrak{u}(N)
sYM theory.
Moreover, it is likely that it is the unique solution which is uniform in
N
.
The existence of this solution is expected on physical grounds because the rank-
2(N{-}1)

\mathfrak{su}(N)
charge lattice is embedded in the rank-
2N
charge lattice of the
\mathfrak{u}(N)
theory in such a way that the induced Dirac pairing on the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
sublattice is the non-principal one given by
J_{2}

Argyres:2022kon
.
