Appendix A
SK structure of an N=4 sYM CB

We recall the definition of an SK structure that defines the CB geometry of a generic
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
SCFT relative to the Kähler structure on the moduli space of vacua.
We then adapt it to the CB geometry of an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory which results in additional constraints on the SK structure due to the simplicity of its moduli space of vacua.
In particular, they are complex orbifolds with isotrivial SK structure and come in one-parameter families with a weak-coupling limit.
For a given
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory with simple gauge algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we claim that such SK structures are determined by

\blacktriangleright

the Dirac pairing,
J
, on the EM charge lattice,

\blacktriangleright

an integral representation,
S
, of the orbifold group which preserves the Dirac pairing, and

\blacktriangleright

a positive definite matrix,
{\bm{\tau}}({\tau})
, that is the low-energy EM coupling matrix and is fixed by the action of this representation of the orbifold group for all values of the complex parameter
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
.

In particular, in this appendix we show that the SK structures of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories are in 1-to-1 correspondence with the orbits of triples
(J,S,{\bm{\tau}})
formed from equivalences that are defined by SK structure isomorphism.
In the body of the paper, we classify all distinct orbits of triples for simple
{\mathfrak{g}}
with principal
J
.

A.1
Review of SK geometry

The Coulomb branch (CB),
{\mathcal{C}}
, is the subset of the moduli space of vacua of 4d
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
supersymmetric theories which have
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
gauge fields coupled to massive charged fields.
17
17

17

Here we mean by
{\mathcal{C}}
the
smooth
locus of the CB, i.e., excluding for the moment subspaces where some charged states become massless on the CB.
We incorporate these massless particle subspaces — which correspond to non-analyticities of the Kähler geometry — after eqn. (
147
) below.

The vevs of the complex scalars in the
r
vector multiplets are coordinates on
{\mathcal{C}}
, and their kinetic terms endow
{\mathcal{C}}
with a Kähler structure in the usual way.
The relation of these scalar fields to the gauge fields via supersymmetry endows
{\mathcal{C}}
with an SK structure.
In particular, the gauge fields couple to massive electrically and magnetically charged states, encoded by the symmetric complex
r\times r
matrix
{\bm{\tau}}
of coefficients of the gauge field kinetic terms.
This is in a basis of gauge fields with respect to which the charges of the massive states span a rank-
2r
lattice,
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}_{e}\oplus{\Lambda}_{m}
, of integer electric and magnetic charges.
This quantization of the charges is due to the Dirac quantization condition, which states that
{\Lambda}
carries a physically observable Dirac pairing,
{\bf j}:{\Lambda}_{e}\times{\Lambda}_{m}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
, which is non-degenerate.

These physical properties of the low energy effective action on the CB can be encoded in an SK geometry of
{\mathcal{C}}
, which is described in a coordinate-invariant way as the
r
-complex-dimensional base of an
algebraic integrable system

({\mathcal{A}},\pi,J,{\Omega})

Donagi:1995cf
;
Freed:1997dp
;
Donagi:1997sr
;
Neitzke:2014cja
.
We review how, with an appropriate choice of bases, the low energy effective action on the CB is derived from the integrable system data.

The complex phase space,
{\mathcal{A}}
, of the integrable system is a connected
2r
-dimensional symplectic manifold with holomorphic symplectic form
{\Omega}
and a proper holomorphic lagrangian fibration
\pi:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.
Its fibers,
A_{u}=\pi^{-1}(u)
for
u\in{\mathcal{C}}
, are abelian varieties, and the fibration being lagrangian means that
{\Omega}|_{A_{u}}=0
.
The rank-
2r
1-homology lattice of the fiber,
{\Lambda}=H_{1}(A_{u})
, is the EM charge lattice of the low-energy theory on the CB.
Since
{\Lambda}
is discrete, it is locally constant, so forms a linear system over
{\mathcal{C}}
which captures the monodromies
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})
it experiences after traversing a linking
1
-cycle
{\gamma}\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}})
in
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Specifically, if we fix a basis
(\widehat{\lambda}_{a},a=1,\ldots,2r)
of the charge lattice at a point
u\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and then drag it around a closed path
{\gamma}\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}})
, it is allowed to come back to itself up to the linear action of the monodromy matrix
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})
,
S_{\mathbb{Z}}({\gamma})\cdot\widehat{\lambda}_{a}\doteq S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})_{a}^{\ b}\widehat{\lambda}_{b}
, that must produce a basis change of
{\Lambda}
, so
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
The set of these monodromies define a
monodromy map

S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}:\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}})\to\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
that produces an integral representation of
\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}})
.
We put the
{\mathbb{Z}}
subscript on the monodromy
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
to emphasize that it defines an integral representation of
\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}})
, and to contrast it with other representations we will introduce shortly.

Furthermore, the fibers come with a choice of a positive polarization,
J\in H^{1,1}(A_{u})\cap H^{2}(A_{u},{\mathbb{Z}})
, which can be viewed as a nondegenerate integral skew-symmetric pairing on the charge lattice,
J:{\Lambda}\times{\Lambda}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
.
As it is discrete, it is also locally constant on
{\mathcal{C}}
like the charge lattice
{\Lambda}
.
But because
J
is identified with the Dirac pairing on the charge lattice in the low-energy theory on the CB, whose value is a physical observable, it must extend to a constant, and therefore global, section over
{\mathcal{C}}
.
So, unlike the charge lattice, this implies it does not experience monodromies, which constrains
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})
to be valued in the subgroup
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

More concretely, define the symplectic form associated to
J
by the
2r\times 2r
matrix with entries
J_{ab}\doteq J(\widehat{\lambda}_{a},\widehat{\lambda}_{b})
relative to a charge lattice basis
(\widehat{\lambda}_{a},a=1,\ldots,2r)
, which we’ll often denote just by
J
when the choice of basis is understood.
Since the polarization
J
is globally defined, this matrix form of
J
must be preserved by the monodromy
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})
associated to a linking
1
-cycle
{\gamma}
:
J\mapsto J^{\prime}=S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})\cdot J\cdot S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})^{t}=J
.
This restricts the image of the monodromy map to
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, the set of automorphisms of the symplectic form
J^{\vee}
,
18
18

18

Note that this definition of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
is convention and defines for us what we call the
symplectic group

\mathrm{Sp}_{X}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
of a generic symplectic form
X
.

\displaystyle S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}

\displaystyle:\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}})\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})

\displaystyle\doteq\{M\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\ |\ M^{t}J^{\vee}M=J^{\vee}\}.

(139)

The image of the monodromy map,
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}))\subset\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, is the
monodromy group
of
{\mathcal{C}}
, which we also denote by
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
or
S
when the context is understood.
That the image of the monodromy map is in the symplectic group of the dual symplectic form
J^{\vee}
to
J
, where
J^{\vee}\doteq J^{-t}
, is an artifact of our definition of the symplectic group of a symplectic form, see footnote
18
.
In particular, if
M
is a basis change of the charge lattice
{\Lambda}
that preserves the matrix form of
J
, then
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}
as opposed to
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}
relative to our definition of the symplectic group
J
.
Note that since the special coordinates are valued in the (complexification of the) dual lattice of the homology (charge) lattice
{\Lambda}
, under a monodromy
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})
they transform linearly under
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}
as opposed to an element of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}
.
Note that if
J
is principal, there is no distinction between
J
and
J^{\vee}
, so
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})=\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})=\mathrm{Sp}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
But for non-principal polarizations
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, though isomorphic as abstract groups, are not integrally equivalent as subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
unless a condition on their invariant factors (that is specified below) is satisfied.
19
19

19

The two matrix groups are related by the inverse transpose of their elements, i.e.
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
iff
M^{-t}\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, which establishes the isomorphism between them as abstract groups.

Because symplectic groups relative to non-principal symplectic forms may be less familiar, we pause here to review them and the way they appear in an SK geometry.
Write an element of the charge lattice as
{\Lambda}\ni{\lambda}=\ell^{a}\widehat{\lambda}_{a}
for some integers
\ell^{a}
— the “charge vector”.
Then the Dirac pairing is

\displaystyle J(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})

\displaystyle\doteq(\ell_{1})^{a}J_{ab}(\ell_{2})^{b}

(140)

thought of as a skew form on the charge vectors.
This is the traditional definition of the
matrix
of the Dirac pairing.
The value of the Dirac pairing between two charges is a physical observable, so the normalization of
J
is physical.
However, the individual matrix elements
J_{ab}
depend on the arbitrary choice of lattice basis.

We can choose a
symplectic basis

({\widehat{m}}_{i},{\widehat{e}}^{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,r
, of
{\Lambda}
such that the sublattices
{\Lambda}_{m}\doteq\langle{\widehat{m}}_{i}\rangle
and
{\Lambda}_{e}\doteq\langle{\widehat{e}}^{i}\rangle
are lagrangian with respect to
J
, i.e.,
J({\widehat{m}}_{i},{\widehat{m}}_{j})=J({\widehat{e}}^{i},{\widehat{e}}^{j})=0
.

J
in this basis is the non-degenerate integral
2r\times 2r
matrix (
5
), i.e.,
J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&{\bf j}\\
-{\bf j}^{t}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, with
({\bf j})^{\ i}_{j}\doteq J({\widehat{m}}_{j},{\widehat{e}}^{i})
.
This symplectic basis can be specialized to
invariant factor form
(a.k.a., Smith normal form),

\displaystyle{\bf j}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}),

\displaystyle d_{i}

\displaystyle\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},

\displaystyle d_{i}|d_{i+1}.

(141)

The
d_{i}
are the
invariant factors
of
J
, and uniquely characterize it.
In particular, there exist change of bases
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
respectively of the
{\Lambda}_{e}
and
{\Lambda}_{m}
sublattices, such that
\mathbf{ujv}=\mathbf{d}
with
\mathbf{d}
of the form (
141
).
Then
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\mathbf{u}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{v}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{t}J\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\mathbf{u}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{v}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\mathbf{d}\\
-\mathbf{d}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

We can define the EM duality monodromies to be either linear transformations acting on the charge vector components as
\ell^{a}\mapsto{N^{a}}_{b}\ell^{b}
, or as linear transformations acting on the components of the special coordinate vector as
{\sigma}_{a}\mapsto{M_{a}}^{b}{\sigma}_{b}
.
Since our main focus is on the special coordinates, we define EM duality transformations to be the linear transformations acting as basis changes on the special coordinate components which also define basis transformations acting on the basis elements of the charge lattice.
The special coordinates are a vector of holomorphic functions on the CB which appear in the central charge of the low energy
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
super Poincaré algebra on the CB as
Z_{\lambda}(u)={\sigma}_{a}\ell^{a}
; we will give a more detailed definition of the special coordinates that connects them to the algebraic integrable system, shortly.
But since the central charge is a physical observable, this implies that the special coordinates are coordinates on the dual vector space to the (complexification of) the charge lattice.
Thus, components of vectors in the special coordinate vector space inherit a symplectic pairing using
J^{\vee}
,

\displaystyle J^{\vee}({\sigma}_{1},{\sigma}_{2})

\displaystyle\doteq({\sigma}_{1})_{a}(J^{\vee})^{ab}({\sigma}_{2})_{b},

\displaystyle J^{\vee}

\displaystyle\doteq J^{-t}.

(142)

Since the monodromies
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\gamma})
preserve the dual symplectic pairing and act as basis changes on the special coordinates components, special coordinate monodromies are in
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
while the charge monodromies are in
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Note, also, that (
139
) incorporates the choice of defining
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
to be the set of
M\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
M^{t}J^{\vee}M=J^{\vee}
rather than
MJ^{\vee}M^{t}=J^{\vee}
.
The second choice is, in our convention, equivalent to the definition of the group
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, as is easily seen by taking the inverse transpose of its defining relation and using the fact that the inverse of any element of a group is also in the group.

Since the definitions of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
(
139
), or the similar definition of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\doteq\{N\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\ |\ N^{t}JN=J\}
, are linear in
J^{\vee}
and
J
, the normalizations of
J
or
J^{\vee}
are irrelevant.
So one can always normalize a rational
J
by dividing by the rational gcd of its elements, so that
J/\mathrm{gcd}(J_{ab})
is an integral symplectic form.
In this case its leading invariant factor in (
141
) is
d_{1}=1
.
The invariant factors,
d^{\vee}_{i}
, of a normalized
J^{\vee}
are related to those of
J
, (
141
), by
d_{i}^{\vee}=d_{r}/d_{r-i+1}
.
So, in general,
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}
and
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}
are not isomorphic over the integers for non-principal
J
.
20
20

20

J
and
J^{\vee}
are isomorphic over the integers if they have the same invariant factors, which happens when
d_{i+1}d_{r-i}=d_{i}d_{r-i+1}
for all
1\leq i\leq r-1
.

Relatedly, for non-principal
J^{\vee}
, in general
M^{t}\notin\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}
when
M\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J}
.

We now return to describing the connection of the algebraic integrable system data to the low energy effective action on the CB.
In a symplectic basis there is always a choice of basis
(\mathrm{d}z_{j})
of
H^{1,0}(A_{u})
such that its period matrix takes the form

\displaystyle\int_{{\widehat{m}}_{i}}\mathrm{d}z_{j}

\displaystyle=({\bm{\tau}}_{u})_{ij},

\displaystyle\int_{{\widehat{e}}^{i}}\mathrm{d}z_{j}

\displaystyle=({\bf j})^{\ i}_{j},

(143)

where
{\bm{\tau}}_{u}
is symmetric and has positive definite imaginary part, so is in the degree-
r
Siegel half space,
{\bm{\tau}}_{u}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
.
This is the content of the Riemann conditions following from
A_{u}
being an abelian variety.
Physically,
{\bm{\tau}}_{u}
is the holomorphically varying matrix of low energy
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
gauge couplings.
The condition that the
A_{u}
fibers are lagrangian implies that

\displaystyle({\bm{\tau}}_{u})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}a^{D}_{i}}{{\partial}a^{j}},

with

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{u}a^{D}_{i}

\displaystyle\doteq\int_{{\widehat{m}}_{i}}{\Omega},

\displaystyle({\bf j}^{t})^{i}_{\ j}\mathrm{d}_{u}a^{j}

\displaystyle\doteq\int_{{\widehat{e}}^{i}}{\Omega},

(144)

where
\mathrm{d}_{u}
is the exterior derivative on
{\mathcal{C}}
and the integrals are fiber-wise integrals of the symplectic form
{\Omega}
, which are well-defined because the fibers are lagrangian.
These fiber periods of
{\Omega}
can be written locally as total derivatives because
{\Omega}
is closed.
In particular,
\{\mathrm{d}_{u}a^{j}\}
forms a basis of
(1,0)
forms at each point on
{\mathcal{C}}
, and in this basis

\displaystyle{\Omega}=\mathrm{d}_{u}a^{j}\wedge dz_{j}.

(145)

For SCFTs, the CB has a complex scale symmetry which fixes only the superconformal vacuum and under which the special coordinates scale homogeneously with weight 1.
Thus the superconformal vacuum is the origin of the special coordinates,

\displaystyle a^{i}=a^{D}_{i}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad i=1,\ldots,r,\qquad\text{at the superconformal vacuum.}

(146)

They can be determined everywhere on
{\mathcal{C}}
by integrating along paths from this origin their differentials given by the fiber periods of
{\Omega}
.
These so-defined (dual) special coordinates on the CB,
a^{j}
(
a^{D}_{i}
), are the vevs of the complex scalar superpartners of the low energy
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
gauge fields.
The
2r
-component vector of dual special coordinates and special coordinates,
(a^{D}_{i},a^{j})
, is what we referred to as the vector of special coordinates
{\sigma}_{a}
in (
142
).
The metric on the CB — describing the kinetic terms of the scalars — is given by

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=(\det{\bf j})\,(\mathrm{Im}{\bm{\tau}}_{u})_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}_{u}a^{i}\overline{\mathrm{d}_{u}a}^{j}.

(147)

In this way we have translated the data of an algebraic integrable system into that of an SK geometry in special coordinates.

Although the above definition treated
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}
as smooth complex manifolds, in fact, the CB, as a metric space, has complex codimension-1 finite-distance non-analyticities along a subvariety
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}
.
21
21

21

All singularities occur in the closure of the codimension-1 singularities for physical reasons described around (
153
) below; see
Argyres:2020wmq
for a detailed description of the stratification of physical SK geometries by singular submanifolds.

The smooth part of
{\mathcal{C}}
, which we now denote
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\doteq{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}
, is what is described by the algebraic integrable system.
In particular, the monodromy map (
139
) is a map from the fundamental group of the non-simply connected
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
.

The image of
{\gamma}\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})
under the monodromy map
S_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is an element of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Write it in terms of
r\times r
block matrices (which we denote by bold letters) as

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{Z}}({\gamma})\doteq\begin{pmatrix}{\bf m}&{\bf n}\\
{\bf p}&{\bf q}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}).

(148)

The definition of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}
in (
139
) implies

\displaystyle({\bf j}^{-1}{\bf m})^{t}{\bf p}

\displaystyle={\bf p}^{t}({\bf j}^{-1}{\bf m}),

\displaystyle({\bf j}^{-1}{\bf n})^{t}{\bf q}

\displaystyle={\bf q}^{t}({\bf j}^{-1}{\bf n}),

\displaystyle{\bf q}^{t}({\bf j}^{-1}{\bf m})-({\bf j}^{-1}{\bf n})^{t}{\bf p}

\displaystyle={\bf j}^{-1}.

(149)

S_{\mathbb{Z}}({\gamma})
acts linearly on the basis of the homology lattice of the fiber (by definition), as well as on the special coordinates (by (
144
), using the invariance of
{\Omega}
) as

\displaystyle S({\gamma})

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}{\widehat{m}}\\
{\widehat{e}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto S_{\mathbb{Z}}({\gamma})\begin{pmatrix}{\widehat{m}}\\
{\widehat{e}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}_{u}a^{D}\\
{\bf j}\,\mathrm{d}_{u}a\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto S_{\mathbb{Z}}({\gamma})\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}_{u}a^{D}\\
{\bf j}^{t}\,\mathrm{d}_{u}a\end{pmatrix},

(150)

in a notation where
{\widehat{m}}
,
{\widehat{e}}
,
a^{D}
, and
a
are treated as
r
-component column vectors.

Define also the following
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
matrices

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{C}}({\gamma})

\displaystyle\doteq{\bf j}^{-t}\bigl({\bf p}({\gamma}){\bm{\tau}}+{\bf q}({\gamma}){\bf j}^{t}\bigr),

\displaystyle S^{\vee}_{\mathbb{C}}({\gamma})

\displaystyle\doteq{\bf j}\bigl({\bf p}({\gamma}){\bm{\tau}}+{\bf q}({\gamma}){\bf j}^{t}\bigr)^{-t}.

(151)

Then the monodromies act on the
dz
basis of
(1,0)
-forms on
A_{u}
, on the special coordinates on
{\mathcal{C}}
, and on
{\bm{\tau}}
as

\displaystyle dz

\displaystyle\mapsto S^{\vee}_{\mathbb{C}}({\gamma})dz,

\displaystyle S({\gamma}):

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{u}a

\displaystyle\mapsto S_{\mathbb{C}}({\gamma})\mathrm{d}_{u}a,

(152)

\displaystyle{\bm{\tau}}

\displaystyle\mapsto S({\gamma})\circ{\bm{\tau}}\doteq({\bf m}{\bm{\tau}}+{\bf n}{\bf j}^{t})({\bf p}{\bm{\tau}}+{\bf q}{\bf j}^{t})^{-1}{\bf j}^{t}.

The last gives a group action on
{\mathscr{H}}_{r}
, while the first two are only group actions if combined with the action on
{\bm{\tau}}
.
These actions follow from the definitions (
143
) and (
144
), and from the
S_{\mathbb{Z}}
actions (
150
).
All these actions of the monodromy group — integral, complex, and Möbius — will play an important role shortly.

There is an additional condition on the special coordinates that stems from the physical origin of the divisor
{\mathcal{D}}
as the locus where the
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
low energy effective action on
{\mathcal{C}}
breaks down due to charged states becoming massless.
The central charge,
Z_{\lambda}(u)
, of the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
Poincaré supersymmetry algebra measures the BPS lower mass bound on states of charge
{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}
at
u\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
Symmetry considerations in a SCFT (where there are no dimensionful parameters and a broken complex scale symmetry) imply that
Z_{\lambda}(u)=m^{t}a^{D}(u)+e^{t}a(u)
in a notation where
m
and
e
are the
r
-component column vectors of magnetic and electric charges relative to the basis
({\widehat{m}}_{i},{\widehat{e}}^{i})
of
{\Lambda}
.
Thus
{\lambda}=m^{i}{\widehat{m}}_{i}+e^{j}{\widehat{e}}_{j}
, and
m^{i},e_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
22
22

22

More invariantly, the
2r
-component complex vector field
(a^{D},a)
is naturally a global section of the vector bundle whose fiber is the complexification of the dual charge lattice,
{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\Lambda}^{*}
, and the central charge is its dual pairing with
{\Lambda}
.

Then the physical requirement is that there must be at least one non-zero charged state becoming massless along each component of
{\mathcal{D}}
,

\displaystyle\forall u

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{D}},

\displaystyle\exists{\lambda}

\displaystyle\in{\Lambda},\quad{\lambda}\neq 0

such that

\displaystyle Z_{\lambda}(u)

\displaystyle=0.

(153)

This is an additional requirement that needs to be imposed, in principle, on the algebraic integrable system data
({\mathcal{A}},\pi,J,{\Omega})
for an SK geometry for it to be physical.
We will see below that this condition is automatically satisfied by the SK structures of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories.

Two monodromy maps,
S
and
S^{\prime}
, which differ just by a choice of charge lattice basis correspond to equivalent SK structures.
Since a change of lattice basis is given by an element
Z\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, we introduce the notion of
integral equivalence
, denoted
\cong_{\mathbb{Z}}
, of representations by

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\cong_{\mathbb{Z}}S^{\prime}

iff

\displaystyle\bigl\{\exists\ Z\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})\

\displaystyle\big|\ ZS({\gamma})=S^{\prime}({\gamma})Z\quad\forall\ {\gamma}\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})\big\}.

(154)

Note that if
S
preserves the symplectic form
J
, then
S^{\prime}
preserves the integrally equivalent symplectic form
J^{\prime}=ZJZ^{t}
.
Analogously, if
S\cong_{{\mathbb{Z}}}S^{\prime}
using
Z\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
and
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
, then
Z\circ{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S^{\prime})
.

{\bm{\tau}}
is only defined up to
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations that correspond to the changes of symplectic basis preserving a given block-skew symplectic form (
5
).
Points of the Siegel upper half-space are identified by the
J^{\vee}
-symplectic action (
A.1
) because they correspond to equivalent SK structures.
Therefore, the points in the quotient space
{{\mathscr{H}}}_{r}/\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
parameterize the inequivalent values for the low-energy couplings
{\bm{\tau}}
.
This space is an
r(r+1)/2
-dimensional connected complex space with orbifold and cusp-like singularities.
Then
[{\bm{\tau}}(u)]
, thought of as the
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
equivalence class of a holomorphic function on
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, defines the holomorphic map

\displaystyle[{\bm{\tau}}]:{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\to{{\mathscr{H}}}_{r}/\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}).

(155)

A.2
N=4 sYM SK structures

{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories with gauge algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
have an exactly marginal gauge coupling,
{\tau}
, and have CBs which are Weyl
({\mathfrak{g}})
orbifolds and are isotrivial.
The SK structure depends on
{\tau}
, which should not be confused with
{\bm{\tau}}\equiv({\bm{\tau}})_{ij}
which is the
r\times r
matrix of low energy
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
couplings.

If the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theory has gauge algebra of rank
r={\mathrm{r}k}({\mathfrak{g}})
with Weyl group acting as the real reflection group
W({\mathfrak{g}})\subset\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
, then its “CB stratum” is an orbifold,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathbb{C}}^{r}/W_{\mathbb{C}},

(156)

with
W_{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}W({\mathfrak{g}})\subset\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
a finite group acting linearly, holomorphically, and faithfully on
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
.
Actually, the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
(
1
) has a slightly different structure.
By decomposing
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
into Higgs, mixed, and Coulomb branches (CBs) with respect to an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
subalgebra of the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
algebra, one finds that the CB stratum is the complex orbifold (
156
), and carries an SK structure.
The triple-SK structure of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is uniquely reconstructed from the SK structure of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
using the
\mathrm{SU}(3)\subset\mathrm{SO}(6)_{R}
symmetry action; see
Argyres:2019yyb
.

Isotrivial
SK geometries
Cecotti:2021ouq
are particularly simple ones in which the map (
155
) is constant.
All
{\mathcal{N}}\geq 3
SCFT moduli spaces are isotrivial by virtue of an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
selection rule
Argyres:1996eh
which says that hypermultiplet effective actions do not depend on vector multiplet scalar vevs; by rotating the choice of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
subalgebra in
{\mathcal{N}}\geq 3
theories, this implies their isotriviality.
There are many
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
SCFT moduli spaces which are isotrivial as well
Cecotti:2021ouq
;
Argyres:2024hdn
.

Constancy of
{\bm{\tau}}
implies it is fixed by the monodromy group action (
A.1
), so

\displaystyle{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{r}.

(157)

\mathrm{Fix}(S)
is determined by the set of quadratic matrix equations

\displaystyle{\bm{\tau}}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)

iff

\displaystyle{\bf m}{\bm{\tau}}+{\bf n}{\bf j}^{t}

\displaystyle={\bm{\tau}}{\bf j}^{-t}({\bf p}{\bm{\tau}}+{\bf q}{\bf j}^{t})

for all

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\bf m}&{\bf n}\\
{\bf p}&{\bf q}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\in S,

(158)

and is connected
Caorsi:2018zsq
.
Note that it is enough to solve (
158
) for a generating set of
S
corresponding to a set of simple reflections that define a generating set of reflections for
W_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
This fixed point set is thus determined by the monodromy group.
If the dimension of the fixed point set is greater than zero, then the geometry is not isolated, and the associated SCFT has a conformal manifold (with singularities) of exactly marginal deformations.
We therefore expect fixed point sets of dimension 1 for
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories.

Furthermore, isotriviality implies the SK metric (
147
) is flat on
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
and the special coordinates are flat coordinates.
Also in
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM theories, the orbifold group acts linearly on the Coulomb branch (special) coordinates as a finite reflection group.
This follows from the existence of a weak coupling limit where the action of the Weyl group gauge identifications on CB vevs are calculable from the classical Higgs mechanism.
The monodromy map actions
S_{\mathbb{C}}
and
S^{\vee}_{\mathbb{C}}
are now representations (group homomorphisms) of
S
and
S^{\vee}
, respectively, by virtue of (
158
).
They are faithful, dual,
r
-complex-dimensional representations of the monodromy group.
Invariance of the symplectic form
{\Omega}
(
145
) requires that the orbifold group acts in the dual (a.k.a., contragredient) representation
W_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
on the abelian variety fiber.
Also, because the CB geometry is isotrivial, the abelian variety fiber is constant, so
A_{u}\doteq A_{\bm{\tau}}
for some fixed
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
.
Thus the orbifold description of the CB extends to an orbifold description of the algebraic integrable system,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}=(A_{\bm{\tau}}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{r})/(W_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}}).

(159)

We now argue that the Weyl group
W_{\mathbb{C}}
action must coincide with the monodromy group
S_{\mathbb{C}}
action on the special coordinates.
This argument has two ingredients.
The first is a general result on the connection between the orbifold group
W_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})
; though elementary, we give a detailed argument for this below, since we do not know where to find it in the literature.
The second is the observation that the monodromy map is trivial in the covering space of the orbifold (
159
), since it is a direct product of the covering space of
{\mathcal{C}}
with a fixed abelian variety.

The singular locus,
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}
, is given by the fixed points of non-identity elements of
W_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Weyl groups are reflection groups, so are generated by reflections, which are elements,
r_{A}\in W_{\mathbb{C}}
, which fix a codimension-1 hyperplane
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{A}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{A}
are the preimages under the orbifold quotient map of the singular points of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Denote their union by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}\doteq\cup_{A}\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{A}
.
The singular locus of the CB is thus
{\mathcal{D}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}/W_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Define
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}\doteq{\mathbb{C}}^{r}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, an
r
-dimensional complex vector space minus some finite number of distinct
(r-1)
-dimensional subspaces.
Then the smooth points of the CB are
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}={\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}/W_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Since we have removed the fixed points,
W_{\mathbb{C}}
acts freely on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}
, and so
{\varpi}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
is a connected cover.
For a point
p\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, pick one preimage
\widetilde{p}\in{\varpi}^{-1}(p)
.
Then
{\varpi}^{-1}(p)
is the discrete set
\{W_{\mathbb{C}}\cdot\widetilde{p}\}\subset{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}
.
By forming the fundamental groups of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
with base points
\widetilde{p}
and
p
, respectively, an elementary argument shows that there is an exact sequence of groups

\displaystyle 1\to\pi_{1}({\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})\xrightarrow{\ {\iota_{\varpi}}\ }\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})\xrightarrow{\ {\phi}\ }\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})/\pi_{1}({\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})\cong W_{\mathbb{C}}\to 1.

(160)

To show this we need that: (1) the map
{\iota_{\varpi}}
induced by the covering map
{\varpi}
is an injective group morphism; and (2) the resulting quotient is isomorphic to the orbifold group
W_{\mathbb{C}}
.

1.

Since
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}
is a cover of
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
,
{\varpi}
maps homotopic paths to homotopic paths, and maps concatenations of paths to concatenations, so
{\iota_{\varpi}}
is a group morphism.
The identity in
\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})
is homotopic to the trivial path based at
p
, and so lifts by
{\varpi}^{-1}
to the trivial path based at
\widetilde{p}
.
Thus
{\iota_{\varpi}}^{-1}(1)=1
in
\pi_{1}({\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})
, and
{\iota_{\varpi}}
is injective.

2.

Any
{\gamma}\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})
lifts to a unique homotopy class of paths
\widetilde{\gamma}
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}
with endpoints
\widetilde{\gamma}(0)=\widetilde{p}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}(1)=g_{\gamma}\cdot\widetilde{p}
for some
g_{\gamma}\in W_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Any representative of coset element
[{\gamma}]\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})/\pi_{1}({\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})
can be written
{\gamma}{\nu}
for some
{\nu}\in\mathrm{im}({\iota_{\varpi}})
.
Its pre-image in
\pi_{1}({\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})
is
{\iota_{\varpi}}^{-1}({\gamma}{\nu})={\iota_{\varpi}}^{-1}({\gamma})\,{\iota_{\varpi}}^{-1}({\nu})=\widetilde{\gamma}\widetilde{\nu}
which is a path starting at
\widetilde{p}
and ending at
g_{\gamma}\cdot\widetilde{p}
.
Furthermore, any path
\widetilde{\delta}
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}
starting at
\widetilde{p}
and ending at
g_{\gamma}\cdot\widetilde{p}
can be written as
\widetilde{\delta}=\widetilde{\gamma}\widetilde{\nu}
with
\widetilde{\nu}\doteq\widetilde{\gamma}^{-1}\widetilde{\delta}\in\pi_{1}({\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})
since it starts and ends at
\widetilde{p}
.
Thus the cosets
[{\gamma}]\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*})/\pi_{1}({\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})
are in 1-to-1 correspondence with elements of
g_{\gamma}\in W_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Because of the direct product structure of the orbifold covering space in (
159
), any closed path
\widetilde{\nu}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
in the
{\mathcal{C}}
orbifold covering space is accompanied by a trivial monodromy on
{\Lambda}
(the 1-homology of
A_{\bm{\tau}}
), and therefore also for the image of this path in the
{\mathcal{C}}
orbifold (
3
).
Thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{im}(S\circ{\iota_{\varpi}})=1,

(161)

the trivial subgroup of
\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
It follows that the monodromy map
S
determines and is determined by an integral symplectic representation of the orbifold group,

\displaystyle W_{\mathbb{Z}}:W_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}).

(162)

W_{\mathbb{Z}}
determines
S
by
S\doteq W_{\mathbb{Z}}\circ{\phi}
, and
S
determines
W_{\mathbb{Z}}
by
W_{\mathbb{Z}}(g)=S\circ{\phi}^{-1}(g)
, where
{\gamma}={\phi}^{-1}(g)
is any representative of the quotient equivalence class.
This makes sense since if
{\gamma}
and
{\gamma}^{\prime}
are in the same class,
{\phi}({\gamma})={\phi}({\gamma}^{\prime})=g
, then
S({\gamma}^{\prime})=S({\gamma}^{\prime}{\gamma}^{-1}{\gamma})=S({\gamma}^{\prime}{\gamma}^{-1})S({\gamma})=S({\gamma})
where we used that
{\gamma}^{\prime}{\gamma}^{-1}\in\ker{\phi}=\mathrm{im}{\iota_{\varpi}}
together with (
161
).

This tells us not only that
S\cong W_{\mathbb{C}}
as abstract groups, but also, since
S
has the linear action
S_{\mathbb{C}}
on the same
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
space of special coordinates, that
S_{\mathbb{C}}\cong_{\mathbb{C}}W_{\mathbb{C}}
.
(Representations are equivalent over the complexes,
{\rho}\cong_{\mathbb{C}}{\rho}^{\prime}
, if there exists a
C\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
such that for all
g\in G
,
{\rho}(g)=C\,{\rho}^{\prime}(g)\,C^{-1}
.)
Although the
S_{\mathbb{C}}
representation (
151
) depends on the value of
{\bm{\tau}}
, it is easy to see that the condition
S_{\mathbb{C}}\cong_{\mathbb{C}}W_{\mathbb{C}}
is satisfied for any
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
as long as

\displaystyle S\cong_{\mathbb{R}}W_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus W_{\mathbb{R}},

(163)

by Matschke’s theorem (representations of a finite group
S
are completely decomposable over
{\mathbb{R}}
) and since
S_{\mathbb{C}}\cong_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}S
and
W_{\mathbb{C}}\cong_{\mathbb{R}}W_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus W_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Thus, the integral symplectic representation of the monodromy group is equivalent over the reals to two copies of the real reflection representation of the orbifold group.

Conversely, since the linear coordinates of the
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
space upon which the Weyl groups acts are the special coordinates, given a choice of
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
, the monodromy action then determines the special coordinates, and so the whole SK geometry of the CB.

A.3
Equivalence of N=4 sYM SK structures

Two integral symplectic representations,
S
and
S^{\prime}
, correspond to equivalent SK structure orbits if they are integrally equivalent,
S\cong_{\mathbb{Z}}S^{\prime}
.
In particular, a map implementing integral equivalence is just a change of choice of basis of the charge lattice fibers, and therefore has no effect on any low energy physical observables on the CB.

But there can be other sources of equivalence between SK structures.
In particular, two CB geometries may be equivalent under a map which has an action on the points of the CB in addition to a change of basis of the homology lattice of the fibers.
To be considered equivalent, all physical observables in the low energy effective action on the CB, or, equivalently, all the coordinate- and basis-independent ingredients of the CB SK structures should be isomorphic.
In coordinate-free language, such an equivalence of SK structures is a polarization- and fiber-preserving holosymplectomorphism of the algebraic integrable system.

Relative to a choice of charge lattice basis (and a choice of base point on the CB), this means that an equivalence of CB geometries is a bijection of the CB to itself,
f:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
, which maps the charge lattice, symplectic pairing, monodromy map, and special coordinates as

\displaystyle f:

\displaystyle\ {\Lambda}_{u}\to{\Lambda}^{\prime}_{u}\doteq Z{\Lambda}_{fu},

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle f:

\displaystyle\ J\to J^{\prime}\doteq Z^{-1}JZ^{-t},

(164)

\displaystyle f:

\displaystyle\ S({\gamma})\to S^{\prime}({\gamma})\doteq ZS(f{\gamma})Z^{-1},

\displaystyle{\gamma}

\displaystyle\in\pi_{1}({\mathcal{C}}^{*}),

\displaystyle f:

\displaystyle\ {\sigma}(u)\to{\sigma}^{\prime}(u)\doteq Z^{\vee}{\sigma}(fu),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{C}},

for some
Z\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
The subscript on
{\Lambda}_{u}
labels the point in
{\mathcal{C}}
over which the charge lattice is a fiber.
Since the charge lattice experiences nontrivial monodromies, (
A.3
) should be understood to apply on simply connected subsets of
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
containing the base point; similarly for the special coordinate maps.
But
Z
implements an “ordinary”
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence (
154
) of SK structures, so, up to an ordinary
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence, we are free to take
Z=\mathbf{1}
.
This then clearly leaves all physical observables — like the EM duality conjugacy classes of the EM monodromies, the matrix
{\bm{\tau}}
of low energy EM couplings, the CB metric, and the
{\mathcal{N}}{=}2
central charge function — invariant.
We refer, somewhat sloppily, to such equivalences which act on the points of the CB as
SK structure isometries
.

In the case of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM CBs, their SK structure isometries are easy to describe explicitly.
We have seen that their CBs are orbifolds by Weyl groups and their monodromy maps are essentially integral symplectic representations of the Weyl group
W
(lifted by the quotient map in (
160
)).
Any automorphism,
{\phi}:W\to W
, of
W
as a reflection group (that is, any group automorphism which maps reflections to reflections) induces a map
f:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
which is an SK structure isometry.
In particular,
{\phi}
determines a
W_{\mathbb{C}}
-equivariant map
\widetilde{f}\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
of the vector space covering the CB orbifold,
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbb{C}}^{r}/W_{\mathbb{C}}
, which descends to the isometry
f:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
upon quotienting by
W_{\mathbb{C}}
.

This follows because, as we review in section
2.6
, all reflection automorphisms of
W
are either inner automorphisms or are Coxeter diagram automorphisms.
Inner automorphisms,
{\phi}(W)=vWv^{-1}
for some
v\in W
, act as
\widetilde{f}=R(v)
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
, where
R:W_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
is a complexified reflection representation of
W
.
(All such representations are equivalent over
{\mathbb{C}}
; different embeddings in
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
differ by linear coordinate changes.)
By taking
R
to be the complexification of one of the integral representations
R_{A}
, it is clear that these inner automorphism SK structure isometries are equivalent, up to a change of coordinates, to “ordinary”
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalences (
154
).

Coxeter diagram automorphisms are defined to be certain permutations of a basis of simple roots of
W
.
23
23

23

For reflection groups, the roots are the directions in
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
perpendicular to the reflection hyperplanes; they are not vectors (with specific norms) as for Lie algebra roots.

Such a permutation of
r
linearly independent lines in
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
can always be implemented by an orthogonal transformation,
\widetilde{f}({\phi})\in\mathrm{O}(r)
, which is interpreted as an element of
\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
when acting on
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}^{r}
.
This definition of
\widetilde{f}({\phi})
is not unique: there are
|W|
choices corresponding to composing any given choice of
\widetilde{f}({\phi})
with all combinations of simple reflections.
This corresponds to composing the Coxeter diagram automorphism with the inner automorphisms.
Note, however, that Coxeter diagram automorphisms need not be inner automorphisms.
Even though both give (complexified) orthogonal actions on
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
, the inner ones are characterized by these transformations being in
W_{\mathbb{C}}
, while it is possible that
\widetilde{f}({\phi})\notin W_{\mathbb{C}}
for the Coxeter diagram automorphisms.
We list the cases in which this occurs in table
4
.

In summary, if
{\phi}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)
is any reflection automorphism of
W
, then there is an associated SK structure isometry, relating two physically indistinguishable CB geometries, but which relates two
a priori
distinct SK structures,

\displaystyle(J,S,{\bm{\tau}})\cong_{\phi}(J,S\circ{\phi},{\bm{\tau}}).

(165)

If
(J,S\circ{\phi},{\bm{\tau}})\cong_{\mathbb{Z}}(J^{\prime},S^{\prime},{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime})
, by combining it with the reflection automorphism isometries we get a potentially larger set of equivalences

\displaystyle(J,S,{\bm{\tau}})\cong_{{\mathbb{Z}},{\phi}}(J^{\prime},S^{\prime},{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}).

(166)

We call this whole set of possible equivalences of
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM CB geometries
automorphism-twisted
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalences
, and they are defined more generally and formally in appendix
B
.
If
{\phi}\in\mathrm{Inn}(W)
is an inner automorphism, then the SK structure isometry
{\mathcal{C}}\cong_{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
can be traded by a change of coordinates for a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence between two associated symplectic representations, so
\mathrm{Inn}(W)
do not give additional equivalences.
But if
{\phi}\in\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)\doteq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{refl.}}(W)/\mathrm{Inn}(W)
, then the associated SK structure isometry gives a new equivalence of geometries whose associated symplectic representations may or may not be integrally equivalent.
(An explicit description of all these equivalences in the
W=BC_{2}
case is given in section
3.3
.)

Thus, we have shown in this appendix that:

The possible SK structures of an
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM CB with Dirac pairing
J
are in 1-to-1 correspondence with automorphism-twisted
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalence classes of pairs
(S,{\bm{\tau}})
with
S
an integral
J^{\vee}
-symplectic representation of the orbifold group satisfying (
163
), and
{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathrm{Fix}(S)
.

Finally, we mentioned earlier that the central charge condition (
153
) is an additional condition that an algebraic integrable system must satisfy for it to describe a physical SK structure.
This condition requires that the monodromy
S({\gamma})
for loops linking
{\mathcal{D}}
fixes integral linear combinations of special coordinates that must vanish there.
But this is automatic for
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sYM orbifold geometries, because the integral symplectic representation
W_{\mathbb{Z}}
acts on the
2r
component vector
(a^{D}\ a)
of (dual) special coordinates, and
{\mathcal{D}}
is the fixed-point locus of this action.
Thus
{\mathcal{D}}
is the union of the eigenvalue
1
eigenspaces of
W_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and since
W_{\mathbb{Z}}
is integral, these eigenspaces are spanned by integral combinations of the (dual) special coordinates.

Appendix B
Integer and rational representations

B.1
Basic definitions

We first introduce the usual notions of finite group representations over rings, and equivalence. In what follows, let
W
be a
finite
group and
R
be a commutative ring (think of
{\mathbb{Z}}
or
{\mathbb{Q}}
).

Definition (
R
-representation).

An
r
-dimensional
R
-representation
of
W
is a group morphism
{\rho}:W\to\mathrm{GL}(r,R)
.

Definition (Reducible and decomposable representations).

An
R
-representation
{\rho}
is
reducible
, respectively
decomposable
, if there exists a matrix
B\in\mathrm{GL}(r,R)
such that for all
w\in W
,
B{\rho}(w)B^{-1}
is block triangular, respectively block diagonal, with at least two non-trivial blocks.

Definition (
R^{\prime}
-equivalence).

Let
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
be a subring of
R
.
Two
R
-representations
{\rho},{\rho}^{\prime}
of
W
are called
R^{\prime}
-equivalent
, denoted
{\rho}\sim_{R^{\prime}}{\rho}^{\prime}
, if there is a matrix
B\in\mathrm{GL}(r,R^{\prime})
such that for all
w\in W
,
B{\rho}^{\prime}(w)={\rho}(w)B
.

We also twist the above definition of equivalence using automorphisms of the group
W
.

Definition (Automorphism-twisted
R^{\prime}
-equivalence).

Let
\Phi\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(W)
be a subgroup of the group of automorphisms of
W
.
Two
R
-representations
{\rho},{\rho}^{\prime}
of
W
are called
(R^{\prime},\Phi)
-equivalent
, denoted
{\rho}\sim_{(R^{\prime},\Phi)}{\rho}^{\prime}
, if there is a matrix
B\in\mathrm{GL}(r,R^{\prime})
, and an automorphism
{\phi}\in\Phi
, such that for all
w\in W
,
B{\rho}^{\prime}(w)={\rho}({\phi}(w))B
.

B.2
Indecomposable reducible representations over
{\mathbb{Z}}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}

We repeatedly use the two following results in the bulk of the paper.

Theorem
(
curtis1966representation
, Th. 73.5)
.

Every
r
-dimensional
{\mathbb{Q}}
-representation of
W
is
{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivalent to an
r
-dimensional
{\mathbb{Z}}
-representation of
W
.

Theorem
(
curtis1966representation
, Th. 73.12)
.

Let
\rho
be a reducible finite-dimensional
{\mathbb{Q}}
-representation of
W
of the form

\displaystyle{\rho}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\rho}_{1}&*\\
0&{\rho}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

(167)

with
{\rho}_{1,2}
some
{\mathbb{Q}}
-representations of
W
.
Then there exist
{\mathbb{Z}}
-representations
\widetilde{\rho}_{1,2}
of
W
such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{i}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}{\rho}_{i}

and

\displaystyle{\rho}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathbb{Z}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\rho}_{1}&*\\
0&{\rho}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

(168)

These follow from Burnside’s argument, which we reproduce here since it is key to the arguments of this paper.

Lemma 1 (Burnside
Burnside:1908
).

Let
G
be a finite subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}(n,{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then there exists a matrix of change of basis
A\in\mathrm{GL}(n,{\mathbb{Q}})
such that
A^{-1}\cdot G\cdot A\subset\mathrm{GL}(n,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Proof.

Let
g_{k}\in\mathrm{GL}(n,{\mathbb{Q}})
be generators of
G
, indexed by
k\in K
, where
K
is a finite set. Let
d
be the greatest common divisor of all the entries of all the matrices
g_{k}
. The matrices
dg_{k}
have only integer entries. Define the lattice

I=\left\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ \Big|\ \forall k\in K\,,\quad g_{k}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\right\}\,.

(169)

Define, for
i=1,\dots,n
,

a_{i,i}=\mathrm{min}\left\{x_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\ \big|\ \exists(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{i-1}\,,\,(x_{1},\dots,x_{i},0,\dots,0)\in I\right\}\,.

(170)

From the construction of
a_{i,i}
, there exists
(a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,i-1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{i-1}
such that

\mathbf{a}_{i}:=(a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,i},0,\dots,0)\in I\,.

(171)

Define then the matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1,1}&a_{2,1}&\cdots&a_{n,1}\\
0&a_{2,2}&\cdots&a_{n,2}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&a_{n,n}\\
\end{pmatrix}\,.

(172)

The matrix
A
has the following crucial property:

X\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad A\cdot X\in I\,.

(173)

The direct implication immediately follows from the lattice structure of
I
. Consider then

x=A\cdot X\in I\,.

(174)

We want to show that
X\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
, and we use induction on the components
X_{i}
of
X
, starting from the last entry
X_{n}
. The
n
-th entry of equation (
174
) gives
x_{n}=a_{n,n}X_{n}
, and by definition of
a_{n,n}
, we have

\frac{x_{n}}{a_{n,n}}=X_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,.

(175)

Assume now that
X_{n},\dots,X_{r+1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then

x-X_{r+1}\mathbf{a}_{r+1}-\dots-X_{n}\mathbf{a}_{n}\in I

(176)

since the
\mathbf{a}_{i}\in I
using (
171
). Moreover only the first
r
entries of that vector are non vanishing, so by definition of
a_{r,r}
we have

\frac{x_{r}-X_{r+1}a_{r+1,r}-\dots-X_{n}a_{n,r}}{a_{r,r}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,.

(177)

This concludes the proof of (
173
), and as a direct consequence,
A^{-1}\cdot g_{k}\cdot A
has integer coefficients, which proves the lemma.

Lemma 2.

Using the notations of the previous lemma, if every
g\in G
has a block diagonal form with blocks of sizes
n_{1},\dots,n_{r}
such that
n_{1}+\dots+n_{r}=n
, then so does the matrix
A
.

Proof.

Assume the block decomposition is given by
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
. The set
I
then splits into
I=I_{1}\oplus I_{2}
. We can then apply the proof of the previous lemma independently on
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, constructing matrices
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. The final matrix
A
has
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
as its two blocks.

B.3
Symplectic representations

Let
J
be a non-degenerate antisymmetric
2r\times 2r
matrix with integer coefficients.
The skew normal form theorem asserts that there exists an integer matrix
U
with determinant 1 such that

J=U^{t}\begin{pmatrix}0&{\delta}\\
-{\delta}&0\end{pmatrix}U

(178)

with
{\delta}=\mathrm{diag}({\delta}_{1},\dots,{\delta}_{r})
and the
{\delta}_{i}
are positive integers such that
{\delta}_{i}|{\delta}_{i+1}
.
These integers are uniquely determined by the matrix
J
.
We say that
J
is principally polarized if
{\delta}_{1}=\dots={\delta}_{r}=1
.
The symplectic group
\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
is the group of
2r\times 2r
matrices
M
such that
M^{t}JM=J
, and we call
J
its
symplectic form
.

Theorem.

Let
W
be a Weyl group of rank
r
.
Let
{\mathcal{R}}:W\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
be a group morphism such that
{\mathcal{R}}\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}R_{1}\oplus R_{2}
with
R_{i}:W\to\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
(for
i=1,2
) two representations which are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivalent to the standard reflection representation of
W
.
Then there exists a matrix
P\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that

P{\mathcal{R}}(w)P^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}S(w)&L(w)\\
0&{\delta}S^{-t}(w){\delta}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

(179)

for some representation
S:W\to\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
and some matrix
L(w)\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and (
179
) is a symplectic representation for the symplectic form
P^{-t}JP^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&{\delta}\\
-{\delta}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Proof.

curtis1966representation
defines a
binding function
for the pair
(R_{1},R_{2})
as a function
L:W\to\mathrm{Mat}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that the mapping

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:W\to\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}R_{1}(w)&L(w)\\
0&R_{2}(w)\end{pmatrix},

(180)

is a group morphism.
This is equivalent to saying that for each
w,w^{\prime}\in W
, we have
L(ww^{\prime})=R_{1}(w)L(w^{\prime})+L(w)R_{2}(w^{\prime})
.
The binding function
L
is
inner
if there exists a matrix
D\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that for all
w\in W
,
L(w)=R_{1}(w)D-DR_{2}(w)
.
Theorem 73.22 in
curtis1966representation
asserts that
|W|L
is an inner binding for any binding
L
.

This guarantees that there exists a matrix
D\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that, if we define

L(w):=\frac{1}{|W|}\left[R_{1}(w)D-DR_{2}(w)\right]\,,

(181)

then
L(w)\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
for all
w\in W
, and
{\mathcal{R}}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalent to the group representation (
180
).
Let
P\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that for all
w\in W
,
{\mathcal{R}}(w)=P^{-1}{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}(w)P
.
Define

J^{\prime}=P^{-t}JP^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
-B^{t}&C\end{pmatrix}

(182)

with
A^{t}=-A
and
C^{t}=-C
.
Then
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}(w)\in\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\prime}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
for all
w\in W
.
This implies

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{1}^{t}(w)AR_{1}(w),

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{1}^{t}(w)AL(w)+R_{1}^{t}(w)BR_{2}(w),

(183)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L^{t}(w)AL(w)-R_{2}^{t}(w)B^{t}L(w)+L^{t}(w)BR_{2}(w)+R_{2}^{t}(w)CR_{2}(w).

We now use the fact that the
R_{i}
are reflection representations of the real Weyl group
W
, so the matrices
R_{i}(w)
are orthogonal.
Intertwiners of orthogonal representations are symmetric so
A=0
, and the last equation in (
B.3
) can be rewritten as

\left[C+\frac{B^{t}D-D^{t}B}{|W|}\right]R_{2}^{-1}(w)=R_{2}^{t}(w)\left[C+\frac{B^{t}D-D^{t}B}{|W|}\right].

(184)

This means the matrix in square brackets is symmetric, so
C=\frac{D^{t}B-B^{t}D}{|W|}
, and we have found that

J^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
-B^{t}&\frac{D^{t}B-B^{t}D}{|W|}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(185)

Now let us put
B
in Smith normal form, i.e., we write
B=U^{t}{\delta}V
with
U,V
integer matrices with determinant 1, and
{\delta}
integer and diagonal with each diagonal entry dividing the next one.
Then define

J^{\prime\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&V\end{pmatrix}^{-t}J^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&V\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\delta}\\
-{\delta}&\frac{(D^{\prime\prime})^{t}{\delta}-{\delta}D^{\prime\prime}}{|W|}\end{pmatrix}

(186)

{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime\prime}(w)=\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&V\end{pmatrix}{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}(w)\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&V\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}R^{\prime\prime}_{1}(w)&L^{\prime\prime}(w)\\
0&R^{\prime\prime}_{2}(w)\end{pmatrix}

(187)

with
R_{1}^{\prime\prime}=UR_{1}U^{-1}
,
R_{2}^{\prime\prime}=VR_{2}V^{-1}
,
D^{\prime\prime}=UDV^{-1}
and
L^{\prime\prime}:=\frac{1}{|W|}\left[R^{\prime\prime}_{1}D^{\prime\prime}-D^{\prime\prime}R^{\prime\prime}_{2}\right]=ULV^{-1}
.
In particular, the middle equation in (
B.3
) becomes

{\delta}=(R_{1}^{\prime\prime})^{t}(w)\,{\delta}\,R^{\prime\prime}_{2}(w)\,.

(188)

We perform one more transformation.
Write the lower right entry of
J^{\prime\prime}
as

\frac{(D^{\prime\prime})^{t}{\delta}-{\delta}D^{\prime\prime}}{|W|}={\Delta}-{\Delta}^{t}\,,

(189)

with
{\Delta}
some matrix with integer coefficients.
Then we transform
J^{\prime\prime}
and
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime\prime}(w)
to

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&{\delta}^{-1}{\Delta}^{t}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-t}J^{\prime\prime}\begin{pmatrix}1&{\delta}^{-1}{\Delta}^{t}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\delta}\\
-{\delta}&0\end{pmatrix},

(190)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime\prime\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&{\delta}^{-1}{\Delta}^{t}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime\prime}(w)\begin{pmatrix}1&{\delta}^{-1}{\Delta}^{t}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}R^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}(w)&L^{\prime\prime\prime}(w)\\
0&R^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2}(w)\end{pmatrix}.

with
R^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}=R^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,
R^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2}=R^{\prime\prime}_{2}
and
D^{\prime\prime\prime}=D^{\prime\prime}-|W|{\delta}^{-1}{\Delta}^{t}
.
This finishes the proof, using (
188
).

Corollary.

Assume
J
is principally polarized.
Any representation
{\mathcal{R}}:W\to\mathrm{Sp}_{J^{\vee}}(2r,{\mathbb{Z}})
of the rank
r
Weyl group
W
which is
{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivalent to two copies of the standard reflection representation is
{\mathbb{Z}}
-equivalent to a representation of the form

\begin{pmatrix}S(w)&L(w)\\
0&S^{-t}(w)\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad L(w)=\frac{1}{|W|}(S(w)D-DS^{-t}(w))\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})

(191)

for some representation
S:W\to\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
and some matrix
D\in\mathrm{GL}(r,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Appendix C
Hecke groups and subgroups

The level-
q
Hecke groups for integer
q>2
are

\displaystyle H_{q}

\displaystyle\doteq\left\langle\textsf{S}_{\ell},\textsf{T}\,|\,\textsf{S}_{\ell}^{2}=(\textsf{S}_{\ell}\textsf{T})^{q}=1\right\rangle,

where

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle\doteq 2[\cos(2\pi/q)+1],

(192)

which act on
{\tau}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
as
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
Möbius transformations
24
24

24

This presentation is equivalent to the ordinary definition of the Hecke groups
Hecke:36
, with generators
\textsf{S},\textsf{T}_{\lambda}
acting as
\textsf{S}:{\tau}^{\prime}\to-1/{\tau}^{\prime}
,
\textsf{T}_{\lambda}:{\tau}^{\prime}\to{\tau}^{\prime}+{\lambda}
, where
{\lambda}\doteq\sqrt{\ell}
, provided we rescale
{\tau}^{\prime}={\lambda}{\tau}
.

\displaystyle\textsf{S}_{\ell}:{\tau}

\displaystyle\mapsto-1/(\ell{\tau}),

\displaystyle\textsf{T}:{\tau}

\displaystyle\mapsto{\tau}+1.

(193)

A double cover in
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
is

\displaystyle\textsf{S}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1/\sqrt{\ell}\\
\sqrt{\ell}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),

T

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,,

(194)

though in this case
\textsf{S}_{\ell}^{2}=(\textsf{S}_{\ell}\textsf{T})^{q}=-1
.
We often use the presentation as a
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
subgroup rather than as a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
one in what follows.

When
q=3,4,6
then
\ell
are the integers
\ell=1,2,3
, respectively.
Note that
H_{3}=\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, while
H_{4}
and
H_{6}
are other inequivalent discrete subgroups of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.
These groups and some of their finite index subgroups appear as S duality groups.
In particular, subgroups of
H_{q}
appear as the S duality groups of theories whose gauge Lie algebra has lacing number

\ell(q)

Dorey:1996hx
;
Argyres:2006qr
;
Aharony:2013hda
.

The proper subgroups which appear are the subgroups of
\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

\displaystyle{\Delta}

\displaystyle\doteq\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\,\Big|\,a+b+d=a+c+d=0\ (\rm{mod}\ 2)\right\}\,,

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{0}(n)

\displaystyle\doteq\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\,\Big|\,c=0\ (\rm{mod}\ n)\right\}\,.

(195)

Also, considered as a projective subgroup,
{\Gamma}_{0}(\ell)\subset H_{q}
is the subgroup generated by
\langle\textsf{S}_{\ell}\textsf{T}\textsf{S}_{\ell},\textsf{T}\rangle
.
Their indices as subgroups of
H_{q}
are computed for general
n
in, for example,
diamond2005first
.
The indices for most of the groups appearing in table
1
are

\begin{array}[]{r|cccc}\text{group}&\ {\Delta}&\ {\Gamma}_{0}(2)&\ {\Gamma}_{0}(3)&\ {\Gamma}_{0}(4)\\
\hline\cr\text{index in }\mathrm{PSL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq H_{3}&2&3&4&6\\
\text{index in }H_{4}&-&2&-&4\\
\text{index in }H_{6}&-&-&2&-\\
\end{array}\ .

(196)
